\nu_{j}^{\bullet}

stands for the function that maps every compact saturated subset
Q_{j}

of
X_{j}
to
\inf_{V}\nu_{j}(V)
, where
V
ranges over the open
neighborhoods
V
of
Q_{j}
.

By equating
{\mathbf{A}}
with the subset
\{0,1/2,1\}
of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and
noting that this identification preserves order, suprema, and infima,
this yields a notion of uniform tightness for
{\mathbf{A}}
-valuations.
Explicitly, and making some simplifications along the way, a family

{(\alpha_{i})}_{i\in I}
of maps from
{\mathcal{O}}{X_{i}}
to
{\mathbf{A}}
is
uniformly tight if and only if for every
i\in I
, for every open
subset
U
of
X_{i}
,

(a)

if
\alpha_{i}(U)=1
then there is a compact saturated
subset
Q
of
X
such that
\upc p_{i}[Q]\subseteq U
and for every

j\in I
with
i\sqsubseteq j
, for every open neighborhood
V
of

\upc p_{j}[Q]
,
\alpha_{j}(V)=1
, and

(b)

if
\alpha_{i}(U)\neq 0
then there is a compact saturated
subset
Q
of
X
such that
\upc p_{i}[Q]\subseteq U
and for every

j\in I
, for every open neighborhood
V
of
\upc p_{j}[Q]
,

\alpha_{j}(V)\neq 0
.

Lemma 8.6

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. Let
(Q_{i},C_{i})
be quasi-lenses on

X_{i}
for each
i\in I
, such that

(Q_{i},C_{i})=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}(Q_{j},C_{j})
for all
i\sqsubseteq j
in

I
. Let
\alpha_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathrm{q}_{X_{i}}(Q_{i},C_{i})
. If

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X,{({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is a projective limit of

{({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
and if
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X,{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is a projective
limit of

{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
, then
{(\alpha_{i})}_{i\in I}
is uniformly tight.

Proof 18

In order to prove
(a)
, we note that since for all

i\sqsubseteq j
in
I
,
(Q_{i},C_{i})=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}(Q_{j},C_{j})
,
we have
Q_{i}={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}(Q_{j})
, by
Lemma
8.3
. Hence
{(Q_{i})}_{i\in I}
is an
element of the canonical projective limit of

{({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
. Since
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X,{({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is another
projective limit, by the universal property of projective limits,
there must be a (unique) element
Q
of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
such that

Q_{i}={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(Q)
for every
i\in I
. Explicitly,

Q_{i}=\upc p_{i}[Q]
for every
i\in I
. Now, let
i\in I
, let

U
be open in
X_{i}
, and let us assume that
\alpha_{i}(U)=1
. By
definition of
\alpha_{i}
as
\mathrm{q}_{X_{i}}(Q_{i},C_{i})
,

Q_{i}\subseteq U
, so
\upc p_{i}[Q]\subseteq U
. For every

j\in I
with
i\sqsubseteq j
and for every open neighborhood
V

of
\upc p_{j}[Q]
, we have

Q_{j}={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{j}}(Q)=\upc p_{j}[Q]\subseteq V
, so

\alpha_{j}(V)=1
.

We turn to
(b)
. Since
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X,{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is a
projective limit of

({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
, we reason as above and we obtain that there is a (unique)
element
C
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
such that
C_{i}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(C)
for every

i\in I
, namely
C_{i}=cl(p_{i}[C])
. Let
i\in I
and
U
be an
open subset of
X_{i}
such that
\alpha_{i}(U)\neq 0
. Since

\alpha_{i}=\mathrm{q}_{X_{i}}(Q_{i},C_{i})
, this means that
C_{i}

intersects
U
, equivalently that
cl(p_{i}[C])
intersects
U
.
Hence
p_{i}[C]
intersects
U
, showing that there is an element

\vec{x}\in C
such that
x_{i}=p_{i}(\vec{x})\in U
. We let

Q\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc\vec{x}
. Then
\upc p_{i}[Q]=\upc x_{i}
is included
in
U
. For every
j\in I
such that
i\sqsubseteq j
, for every
open neighborhood
V
of
\upc p_{j}[Q]
,
V
contains

x_{j}=p_{j}(\vec{x})
. But
x_{j}
is in
p_{j}[C]
, hence in
C_{j}
, so

C_{j}
intersects
V
, and therefore
\alpha_{j}(V)\neq 0
. ∎

The point of uniform tightness stems from Lemma 6.5 of

[
20
]
. For every
\mu\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
there is map
\mu^{\circ}\colon{\mathcal{O}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defined by

\mu^{\circ}(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{Q}\mu(Q)
, where
Q
ranges over the
compact saturated subsets of
X
included in
U
. (Beware that

{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}X
was written as
{\mathcal{Q}}X
in
[
20
]
.)
Given any projective system

{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
of topological
spaces, given Scott-continuous maps
\nu_{i}\colon{\mathcal{O}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

for each
i\in I
, such that
\nu_{i}=p_{ij}[\nu_{j}]
for all

i\sqsubseteq j\in I
, one can define a map

\mu\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
by

\mu(Q)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\inf\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{i\in I}\nu_{i}^{\bullet}(\upc p_{i}[Q])
; the
arrow superscript denotes the fact that the infimum is filtered, in
fact
i\sqsubseteq j\in I
implies

\nu_{i}^{\bullet}(\upc p_{i}[Q])\geq\nu_{j}^{\bullet}(\upc p_{j}[Q])
.
Then Lemma 6.5 of
[
20
]
states the equivalence between
three conditions, among which the following two: (1)

{(\nu_{i})}_{i\in I}
is uniformly tight, (3) for every
i\in I
,

\nu_{i}=p_{i}[\mu^{\circ}]
. This applies verbatim to
{\mathbf{A}}
-valuations

\alpha_{i}
for
\nu_{i}
, modulo our identification of
{\mathbf{A}}
with

\{0,1/2,1\}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

Proposition 8.7

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. Let
\alpha_{i}
be
{\mathbf{A}}
-valuations on
each
X_{i}
such that
\alpha_{i}=p_{ij}[\alpha_{j}]
for all
i\sqsubseteq j\in I
. If
{(\alpha_{i})}_{i\in I}
is
uniformly tight, then there is a Scott-continuous map
\alpha\colon{\mathcal{O}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that
for every
i\in I
,
\alpha_{i}=p_{i}[\alpha]
, and
\alpha
is an

{\mathbf{A}}
-valuation.

Proof 19

We define
\mu\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}X\to{\mathbf{A}}
by

\mu(Q)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\inf\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}^{\bullet}(\upc p_{i}[Q])
for
every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}X
. Since
{(\alpha_{i})}_{i\in I}
is
uniformly tight by assumption, Lemma 6.5 of
[
20
]
,
as discussed above, entails that
\alpha_{i}=p_{i}[\mu^{\circ}]
for
every
i\in I
.

We claim that
\mu^{\circ}
is Scott-continuous. We recall that

\mu^{\circ}(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{Q}\mu(Q)
, where
Q
ranges over the
compact saturated subsets of
X
included in
U
, for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. It is easy to see that
\mu^{\circ}
is monotonic.
Let
{(U_{j})}_{j\in J}
be a directed family of open subsets of
X
,
with union equal to
U
. Since
\mu^{\circ}
is monotonic,

\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{j\in J}\mu^{\circ}(U_{j})\leq\mu^{\circ}(U)
. In order to
show the reverse inequality, it suffices to show that for every

r<\mu^{\circ}(U)
, there is an index
j\in J
such that

r\leq\mu^{\circ}(U_{j})
. Since
r<\mu^{\circ}(U)
, by definition
of
\mu^{\circ}
, there is a compact saturated subset
Q
of
X

included in
U
such that
r\leq\mu(Q)
. Since
Q
is compact
and
{(U_{j})}_{j\in J}
is directed,
Q\subseteq U_{j}
for some

j\in J
, and therefore
r\leq\mu^{\circ}(U_{j})
.

The map
\mu^{\circ}
is strict: the only compact saturated set
included in
\emptyset
is the empty set, and

\mu(\emptyset)=\inf\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{i\in I}\nu_{i}^{\bullet}(\upc p_{i}[\emptyset])=\inf\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{i\in I}\nu_{i}^{\bullet}(\emptyset)
; this is
equal to
0
, because for every
i\in I
,

\nu_{i}^{\bullet}(\emptyset)
is the infimum of the values

\nu_{i}(V)
, where
V
ranges over the open neighborhoods of

\emptyset
, namely
\nu_{i}(\emptyset)=0
.

We show that
\mu^{\circ}
satisfies the remaining defining conditions
for an
{\mathbf{A}}
-valuation. For short, we will write
U_{i}
for the
largest open subset of
X_{i}
such that
p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})\subseteq U
,
for every open subset
U
of
X
; and similarly
V_{i}
for
V
, for
example. Since
U=\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})
, and since

\mu^{\circ}
is Scott-continuous, we have

\mu^{\circ}(U)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\mu^{\circ}(p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i}))=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}(U_{i})
.

When
U=X
, the sets
U_{i}
are equal to the given spaces
X_{i}
, so

\mu^{\circ}(X)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}(X_{i})=1
.

Let
U
and
V
be arbitrary open subsets of
X
. If

\mu^{\circ}(U)=0
, then
\alpha_{i}(U_{i})=0
for every
i\in I
. We
have
U\cup V=\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i}\cup V_{i})
, so

\mu^{\circ}(U\cup V)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\mu^{\circ}(p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i}\cup V_{i}))
(since
\mu^{\circ}
is Scott-continuous)

=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}(U_{i}\cup V_{i})
. That is equal to

\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}(V_{i})
by Condition 1 of the definition of

{\mathbf{A}}
-valuations; so
\mu^{\circ}(U\cup V)=\mu^{\circ}(V)
, provided
that
\mu^{\circ}(U)=0
.

If
\mu^{\circ}(V)=1
, then
\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}(V_{i})=1
, and
since
\alpha_{i}(V_{i})
can only take the values
0
,
\mathbf{M}

(
=1/2
) and
1
, we must have
\alpha_{i_{0}}(V_{i_{0}})=1
for some

i_{0}\in I
. Then
\alpha_{i}(V_{i})=1
for every
i\sqsupseteq i_{0}
,
since then
p_{i_{0}}^{-1}(V_{i_{0}})\subseteq p_{i}^{-1}(V_{i})
, and
then

1=\alpha_{i_{0}}(V_{i_{0}})=\mu^{\circ}(p_{i_{0}}^{-1}(V_{i_{0}}))\leq\mu^{\circ}(p_{i}^{-1}(V_{i}))=\alpha_{i}(V_{i})
. We observe that

U\cap V=\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i}\cap V_{i})
: every point of

U\cap V
is in
p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})
for some
i\in I
, in

p_{j}^{-1}(V_{j})
for some
j\in I
, hence in

p_{k}^{-1}(U_{k})\cap p_{k}^{-1}(V_{k})=p_{k}^{-1}(U_{k}\cap V_{k})
for
some
k\in I
such that
i,j\sqsubseteq k
; the reverse inclusion
is obvious. Since
\mu^{\circ}
is Scott-continuous, it follows that

\mu^{\circ}(U\cap V)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}(U_{i}\cap V_{i})
.
For every
i\sqsupseteq i_{0}
,

\alpha_{i}(U_{i}\cap V_{i})=\alpha_{i}(U_{i})
by Condition 2 of the
definition of
{\mathbf{A}}
-valuations; so

\mu^{\circ}(U\cap V)\geq\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\sqsupseteq i_{0}}\alpha_{i}(U_{i})=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}(U_{i})
(since the family of indices

i\sqsupseteq i_{0}
is cofinal in
I
)
=\mu^{\circ}(U)
. The
reverse inequality follows by monotonicity of
\mu^{\circ}
. ∎

Putting everything together, we obtain the following.

Theorem 8.8

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. If every
X_{i}
is sober,
and if
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
is a projective limit of

{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
, then
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
is a projective limit of

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
and
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
is a projective limit of

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
.

Proof 20

Let
Z^{\sharp}
be the canonical projective limit of

({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
and
\varphi^{\sharp}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to Z^{\sharp}
be the
comparison map;
\varphi^{\sharp}
is a homeomorphism by
Theorem
6.4
. Let
Z^{\flat}
be the canonical
projective limit of

({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
and
\varphi^{\flat}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to Z^{\flat}
be the comparison
map;
\varphi^{\flat}
is a homeomorphism by assumption. Finally, let

Z^{\natural}
be the canonical projective limit of

(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
and
\varphi^{\natural}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to Z^{\natural}
be the
comparison map. Considering Proposition
8.4
,
\varphi^{\natural}
is a topological embedding, and we need to show
that it is surjective.

Let
(Q_{i},C_{i})\in\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}}
be given for each
i\in I
so that
for all
i\sqsubseteq j\in I
,

(Q_{i},C_{i})=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}(Q_{j},C_{j})
. Let

\alpha_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathrm{q}_{X_{i}}(Q_{i},C_{i})
. Both
\varphi^{\sharp}

and
\varphi^{\flat}
are homeomorphisms, so we can apply
Lemma
8.6
and conclude that

{(\alpha_{i})}_{i\in I}
is uniformly tight. By naturality of

\mathrm{q}_{\_}
(Lemma
8.5
), we have

\alpha_{i}=p_{ij}[\alpha_{j}]
for all
i\sqsubseteq j\in I
, so
Proposition
8.7
applies, giving us an

{\mathbf{A}}
-valuation
\alpha
on
X
such that
\alpha_{i}=p_{i}[\alpha]

for every
i\in I
. Any limit of sober spaces, taken in
\mathbf{Top}
,
is sober
[
18
, Theorem 8.4.13]
. Therefore
X
is
sober, and because of that,
\mathrm{q}_{X}
is a homeomorphism. In
particular
\alpha=\mathrm{q}_{X}(Q,C)
for some unique quasi-lens

(Q,C)
on
X
. For every
i\in I
, the fact that

\alpha_{i}=p_{i}[\alpha]
entails that

\mathrm{q}_{X_{i}}(Q_{i},C_{i})=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(\mathrm{q}_{X}(Q,C))
, which is
equal to
\mathrm{q}_{X_{i}}(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(Q,C))
, by naturality of

\mathrm{q}_{\_}
(Lemma
8.5
). Since each
X_{i}
is
sober,
\mathrm{q}_{X_{i}}
is a homeomorphism, so

(Q_{i},C_{i})=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(Q,C)
.

The case of
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}
is an immediate consequence, since
\mathrm{q}_{X}

and the maps
\mathrm{q}_{X_{i}}
are homeomorphisms, and using the
naturality of
\mathrm{q}_{\_}
once again. ∎

Example 8.9

Sobriety is required in Theorem
8.8
. Let us look
back at Stone’s counterexample
6.6
. Each
X_{n}
is
compact, so
(X_{n},X_{n})
is a quasi-lens. For all

m\leq n\in\mathbb{N}
,
p_{mn}\colon X_{n}\to X_{m}
is the identity
map, so
(X_{m},X_{m})=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{mn}}(X_{n},X_{n})
. It follows that

{(X_{n},X_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an element of the projective limit

Z
of

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{mn}}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{n}}\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{m}})}_{m\leq n\in\mathbb{N}}
. But it is not in the image of the comparison map

\varphi\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to Z
. Indeed,
X
is
\mathbb{N}
with the
discrete topology, so the only quasi-lenses on
X
are the pairs

(A,A)
where
A
is a non-empty finite subset of
\mathbb{N}
,
and their images by
\varphi
are the constant
\mathbb{N}
-indexed tuples
whose entries are all equal to
(A,A)
.

Remark 8.10

The requirement that
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
be a projective limit of

({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon\allowbreak{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
in Theorem
8.8
cannot be
dispensed with if every
X_{i}
is not only sober but also pointed and
if the bonding maps
p_{ij}
are strict. A
pointed
space is
a space with a least element
\bot
in its specialization ordering,
or equivalently with an element whose sole open neighborhood is the
whole space; every pointed space is compact. A
strict
map is
a function that maps least elements to least elements. In that
case, the projective limit is also pointed. In a compact space, for
every non-empty closed subset
C
,
(\upc C,C)
is a quasi-lens; we
even have
C\subseteq cl(\upc C\cap C)
, from which it is
immediate to see that every open neighborhood
U
of
\upc C

satisfies
C\subseteq cl(U\cap C)
. In a pointed space
Y
,
C

contains
\bot
, so
\upc C
is simply the whole space
Y
. Now let
us use the notations of the proof of Theorem
8.8
,
and let us assume that each
X_{i}
is sober and pointed, and that
each
p_{ij}
is strict. Let
{(C_{i})}_{i\in I}
be any element of

Z^{\flat}
. Then each pair
(X_{i},C_{i})
is a quasi-lens, as we have
just seen. For all
i\sqsubseteq j\in I
,

\upc X_{i}=\upc{p_{ij}}[\upc X_{j}]
because every element of

\upc X_{i}=X_{i}
is larger than or equal to its bottom element, which
is obtained as the image of the bottom element of
X_{j}
by the
strict function
p_{ij}
. It follows that
{(X_{i},C_{i})}_{i\in I}

is in
Z^{\natural}
. If
\varphi^{\natural}
is surjective, then there
is a quasi-lens
(Q,C)
on
X
such that

(X_{i},C_{i})=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(Q,C)
for every
i\in I
, in particular
such that
C_{i}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(C)
for every
i\in I
. Therefore

\varphi^{\flat}
must be surjective, too, hence a homeomorphism, by
Proposition
7.1
.

We combine Theorem
8.8
with
Theorem
7.5
, and we obtain the following. We remember
from Remark
7.7
that every projection is proper.

Corollary 8.11

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. If every
X_{i}
is sober, and:

1.

every
p_{ij}
is a proper map (e.g., a projection),

2.

or
I
has a countable cofinal subset and each
X_{i}
is
locally compact,

then
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
is a projective limit of

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
and
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
is a projective limit of

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
.

The assumptions of Corollary
8.11
are not quite
tight, as the following corner case demonstrates.

Remark 8.12

The fact that
X_{i}
be sober is not needed when

{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
is the
projective system underlying an ep-system, and in the case of the

\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}
functor. Explicitly, let

(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.19936pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.19936pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.32152pt\raise 9.7986pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.52084pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{e_{ij}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.19936pt\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.19936pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.15741pt\raise-9.79858pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.52084pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p_{ij}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.19936pt\raise-4.30554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be an ep-system,
with canonical projective limit
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. Let
Z
be
the canonical projective limit of

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
. Then the comparison map

\varphi\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to Z
is a homeomorphism. This is a
consequence of Proposition
5.1
and the fact that

\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathsf{V}}Y
is sober for every space
Y

[
29
, Theorem 3.2]
, hence a monotone convergence
space, and certainly a
T_{0}
space.

Example 8.13

Example
7.13
(where each
X_{n}
is defined as

\mathbb{Q}_{\bot}\smallsetminus\{q_{0},\cdots,q_{n-1}\}
) gives an example of a
projective system of compact sober spaces, with a countable index
set, whose projective limit is not preserved by
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
, by
Remark
8.10
. Hence sobriety is not enough in
case 2 of Corollary
8.11
. We recall that sobriety
itself is needed, see Example
8.9
.

9
Lenses

The study of lenses will require us to talk about weakly Hausdorff
spaces, and about quasi-Polish spaces.

A topological space
X
is
weakly Hausdorff
in the sense of
Lawson and Keimel
[
35
, Lemma 6.6]
if and only if for
all
x,y\in X
, every open neighborhood
W
of
\upc x\cap\upc y

contains an intersection
U\cap V
of an open neighborhood
U
of
x

and of an open neighborhood
V
of
y
, equivalently if for all
compact saturated subsets
Q
,
Q^{\prime}
of
X
, every open neighborhood

W
of
Q\cap Q^{\prime}
contains an intersection
U\cap V
of an open
neighborhood
U
of
Q
and of an open neighborhood
V
of
Q^{\prime}
. All
Hausdorff spaces, all stably locally compact spaces are weakly
Hausdorff; see
[
22
]
for further information.

Quasi-Polish spaces were invented by M. de Brecht

[
5
]
, and can be characterized in many ways. The
original definition is as the topological spaces obtained from
second-countable Smyth-complete quasi-metric spaces in their open ball
topology, just keeping the topology and throwing away the
quasi-metric. A space is
second-countable
if and only if it
has a countable base. We will not need to refer to quasi-metric
spaces or Smyth-completeness in the sequel, so we omit the
definitions. Every Polish space is quasi-Polish, and also every

\omega
-continuous dcpo from domain theory.

The latter are defined as follows. In a dcpo
P
, let
x\ll y

(“
x
is
way below

y
”) if and only if every directed family

D
such that
y\leq\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}D
contains an element
d\in D
such that

x\leq d
. A
basis
for
P
is a a subset
B
such that, for
every
x\in P
,
\hbox to0.0pt{$\downarrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\downarrow$}_{B}x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{b\in B\mid b\ll x\}
is
directed and has
x
as its supremum. A dcpo is
continuous
if
and only if it has a basis, and
\omega
-continuous
if and only
if it has a countable basis. For example, for any set
I
, the dcpo

\mathbb{P}(I)
, ordered by inclusion, is continuous with basis

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(I)
. It is
\omega
-continuous if
I
is countable. (That is
in fact an example of an
algebraic
domain, where the set of

finite elements

\{x\in P\mid x\ll x\}
serves as a basis.
Every algebraic domain is continuous.) In a continuous dcpo
P
with
basis
B
, the sets
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}b\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{x\in P\mid b\ll x\}
,

b\in B
, form a base of the Scott topology.

We recall that a
G_{\delta}
subset of a topological space
X
is a
countable intersection of open subsets. A
\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
subset
of

X
is a countable intersection of UCO subsets, where a
UCO
subset
of
X
is a set of the form
U\mathrel{\Rightarrow}V
, denoting

\{x\in X\mid\text{if }x\in U\text{ then }x\in V\}
, with

U,V\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. Every open subset is UCO, so every
G_{\delta}

subset is
\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
. The
\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
subsets are crucial in
understanding the structure of quasi-Polish spaces as, notably, the
subspaces of a quasi-Polish space that are quasi-Polish are exactly
its
\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
subsets
[
5
, Corollary 23]
.

Proposition 9.1

The following are equivalent for a topological space
X
:

1.

X
is quasi-Polish;

2.

X
is homeomorphic to a
\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
subspace of an

\omega
-continuous dcpo;

3.

X
is homeomorphic to a
G_{\delta}
subspace of an

\omega
-continuous dcpo.

The
\omega
-continuous dcpo is given its Scott topology, and the

G_{\delta}
or the
\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
subspace has the subspace topology; mind
that the latter is not a Scott topology in general.

Proof 21

3\mathrel{\Rightarrow}2\mathrel{\Rightarrow}1
. Every
G_{\delta}
subspace
X
of an

\omega
-continuous dcpo
P
is in particular a
\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
subspace
of
P
. Every
\omega
-continuous dcpo is quasi-Polish

[
5
, Corollary 45]
, hence so is any of its

\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{0}_{2}
subspace.

1\mathrel{\Rightarrow}3
. If
X
is quasi-Polish, then
X
has an

\omega
-quasi-ideal model
[
26
, Theorem 8.18]
. An

\omega
-quasi-ideal domain is an algebraic domain with countably
many finite elements, and in which every element smaller than or
equal to a finite element is itself finite

[
26
, Definition 8.1]
. Clearly every

\omega
-quasi-ideal domain is
\omega
-continuous. An

\omega
-quasi-ideal
model
of a space
X
is an

\omega
-quasi-ideal domain
P
such that
X
is homeomorphic to the
subspace of non-finite elements of
P
. Listing the finite elements
of
P
as
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
, …,
p_{n}
, …, the sets
\dc p_{n}

are closed, so their complements
U_{n}
are open, and the set of
non-finite elements of
P
is
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}U_{n}
, hence a

G_{\delta}
subset of
P
. ∎

The point in introducing those kinds of spaces is that the space of
lenses
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}X
is naturally isomorphic to the space of
quasi-lenses
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
, provided that
X
is weakly Hausdorff or a
quasi-Polish spaces, as we are going to argue.

Lemma 6.1, Proposition 6.2 and Theorem 6.3 and of
[
22
]

state the following, among other things.

Lemma 9.2
(
[
22
]
)

For every topological space
X
, there is a topological embedding

\iota_{X}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}X\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
, defined by

\iota_{X}(L)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\upc L,cl(L))
, and we have

{(\iota_{X})}^{-1}(\Box^{\mathrm{q}}U)=\Box U
and

{(\iota_{X})}^{-1}(\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}U)=\Diamond U
for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. There is a map

\varrho_{X}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}X
defined by

\varrho_{X}(Q,C)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}Q\cap C
, and

\varrho_{X}\circ\iota_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X}
. If
X
satisfies the
following property:

(*)
for every compact saturated subset
Q
of
X
, for every
closed subset
C
of
X
, if
C\subseteq cl(U\cap C)
for every
open neighborhood
U
of
Q
, then
C\subseteq cl(Q\cap C)
,

then
\iota_{X}
is a homeomorphism, with inverse
\varrho_{X}
.

Property
(*)
is satisfied, in particular, if
X
is weakly
Hausdorff.

We turn to quasi-Polish spaces, and for that we examine

\omega
-continuous dcpos first. For a finite set
E
, we write

\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E
for
\bigcup_{x\in E}\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x
. The notation
\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}

refers to the intersection of a filtered family. The following lemma
is folklore.

Lemma 9.3

Let
X
be an
\omega
-continuous dcpo, with a countable basis
B
.
For every compact saturated subset
Q
of
X
, there is a sequence
of finite subsets
E_{n}
of
B
such that

E_{n+1}\subseteq\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and such
that

Q=\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n}=\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\upc E_{n}
.

Proof 22

Let us first observe that for every open neighborhood
U
of
Q
,
there is a finite subset
E
of
B
such that

Q\subseteq\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E\subseteq\upc E\subseteq U
. Indeed, for
each
x\in Q
, there is a point
b_{x}\in B
such that
b_{x}\in U

and
b_{x}\ll x
. Then
{(\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}b_{x})}_{x\in Q}
is an open cover
of
Q
. We extract a finite subcover
{(\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}b_{x})}_{x\in E}
,
and then
Q\subseteq\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E\subseteq\upc E\subseteq U
, as
desired.

Since
Q
is saturated,
Q
is also equal to the intersection of its
open neighborhoods, hence also to the intersection

\bigcap\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E
, where
E
ranges over the finite subsets of

B
such that
Q\subseteq\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E
, by the observation we have
just made. Since
B
is countable, there are countably many such
sets
E
. Let us enumerate them as
E^{0}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We
define a finite subset
E_{n}
of
B
by induction on
n
in such a
way that
Q\subseteq\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n}
and

E_{n+1}\subseteq\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
as follows.
First,
E_{0}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}E^{0}_{0}
. Then, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we let

E_{n+1}
be any finite subset of
B
such that

Q\subseteq\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n+1}\subseteq\upc E_{n+1}\subseteq\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E^{0}_{n+1}\cap\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n}
, using our preliminary
observation. We have

Q\subseteq\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n}\subseteq\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\upc E_{n}\subseteq\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E^{0}_{n}=Q
,
whence the claim. ∎

Lemma 9.4

Every
\omega
-continuous dcpo
P
(with its Scott topology)
satisfies Property
(*)
.

Proof 23

We fix a compact saturated subset
Q
of
P
and a closed subset
C

of
P
, such that for every open neighborhood
U
of
Q
,
C\subseteq cl(U\cap C)
.

Let
B
be a countable basis of
P
, and
E_{n}
be as given in
Lemma
9.3
. Let
x
be any point of
B
such
that
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x
intersects
C
. We build a monotone sequence of
points
{(x_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
x_{n}\in B\cap\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n}

and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x_{n}
intersects
C
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This is
by induction on
n
. Since
Q\subseteq\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{0}
, by
assumption
C\subseteq cl(\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{0}\cap C)
. Since

\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x
intersects
C
, it also intersects

\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{0}\cap C
. Let
y
be any point in the intersection.
Since
y
is in the Scott-open set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x\cap\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{0}
,
there is a point
x_{0}
of
B
in
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x\cap\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{0}
such
that
x_{0}\ll y
. In particular,
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x_{0}
intersects
C
(at

y
), and
x_{0}
is in
B\cap\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{0}
. This starts the
induction. Given that
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x_{n}
intersects
C
, we proceed in
the same way to obtain
x_{n+1}
. Since
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x_{n}
intersects

C
and
Q\subseteq\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n+1}
,
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x_{n}
also
intersects
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n+1}\cap C
; we pick
y
in the
intersection, and
x_{n+1}\in B
such that
x_{n+1}\ll y
and

x_{n+1}\in\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x_{n}\cap\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n+1}
.

Let
x_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}x_{n}
. Since
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x_{n}

intersects
C
and
C
is downwards-closed,
x_{n}
is itself in
C

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, so
x_{\infty}
is in
C
. Since

x_{n}\in\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n}
for every
n
, hence also

x_{\infty}\in\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}E_{n}
,
x_{\infty}
is in
Q
, using
Lemma
9.3
. Hence
x_{\infty}
is in
Q\cap C
.
Additionally,
x\ll x_{0}\leq x_{\infty}
. Therefore we have shown
that for every
x\in B
, if
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x
intersects
C
then it
also intersects
Q\cap C
. Since every Scott-open set is a union
of sets of the form
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x
with
x\in B
, every open set that
intersects
C
also intersects
Q\cap C
. We conclude that

C\subseteq cl(Q\cap C)
. ∎

Proposition 9.5

Every quasi-Polish space satisfies Property
(*)
.

Proof 24

Let us equate
X
with a
G_{\delta}
subspace of an

\omega
-continuous dcpo
Y
, thanks to
Proposition
9.1
. Let us write
\upc_{X}
,

\upc_{Y}
for upward closures in
X
, resp.
Y
, and let us note
that
\upc_{Y}A=\upc_{X}A
for every subset
A
of
X
, because
X

is upwards-closed in
Y
. Let us also write
cl_{X}
,
cl_{Y}
for
closure in
X
, resp.
Y
, and let us note that

cl_{X}(A)=cl_{Y}(A)\cap X
for every subset
A
of
X
.

Let
Q
be a compact saturated subset of
X
, let
C
be a closed
subset of
X
, and let us assume that for every open neighborhood

U
of
Q
,
C\subseteq cl_{X}(U\cap C)
. We let

C^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}cl_{Y}(C)
, and we claim that for every open neighborhood

V
of
Q
in
Y
,
C^{\prime}\subseteq cl_{Y}(V\cap C^{\prime})
. Let

U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}V\cap X
, an open neighborhood of
Q
in
X
. By
assumption,
C\subseteq cl_{X}(U\cap C)
, in particular

C\subseteq cl_{Y}(U\cap C)
, and since

U\cap C=V\cap X\cap C=V\cap C\subseteq V\cap C^{\prime}
,

C\subseteq cl_{Y}(V\cap C^{\prime})
. Taking closures in
Y
,

C^{\prime}\subseteq cl_{Y}(V\cap C^{\prime})
.

We note that
Q
is compact saturated in
X
, hence in
Y
, and that

C^{\prime}
is closed in
Y
. By Lemma
9.4
,
Y

satisfies Property
(*)
, so
C^{\prime}\subseteq cl_{Y}(Q\cap C^{\prime})
. But

Q\cap C^{\prime}=Q\cap X\cap C^{\prime}=Q\cap C
, so

C=C^{\prime}\cap X\subseteq cl_{Y}(Q\cap C)\cap X=cl_{X}(Q\cap C)
.
∎

Theorem 9.6

For every quasi-Polish space
X
, the spaces
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
and

\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}X
are quasi-Polish, and homeomorphic through
\iota_{X}

and
\varrho_{X}
.

Proof 25

Theorem 5.1 of
[
7
]
states that, when
X
is
quasi-Polish, so is
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}X
. By
Proposition
9.5
,
X
satisfies Property
(*)
,
so we may apply Lemma
9.2
and conclude. ∎

Figure 1:
Knijnenburg’s dcpo

Remark 9.7

One may wonder whether every quasi-Polish space would simply just be
weakly Hausdorff, in which case Theorem
9.6
would
be implied by Lemma
9.2
. That is not true. Consider
the dcpo of Figure
1
, due to Peter Knijnenburg

[
39
, Example 6.1]
. (We have only removed
the bottom element from the original example.) Its elements are

a_{n}
and
b_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
, and
c_{n}

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
(not
\omega
), all pairwise distinct. The
ordering is given by:
a_{m}\leq a_{n}
,
b_{m}\leq b_{n}
,

a_{m}\leq c_{n}
,
b_{m}\leq c_{n}
if and only if
m\leq n
; all other
pairs of elements are incomparable. This is an
\omega
-continuous
dcpo, even an
\omega
-algebraic dcpo, whose finite elements are all
elements except
a_{\omega}
and
b_{\omega}
. Every open neighborhood
of
a_{\omega}
intersects every open neighborhood of
b_{\omega}
, so
it is not weakly Hausdorff. One may also note that, in a weakly
Hausdorff space, for every lens
L
,
\dc L=cl(L)

[
22
, Theorem 6.4]
, and that is not the case here, as
Knijnenburg notices:

L\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{c_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{b_{\omega}\}
is a non-empty
compact saturated subset, hence a lens, but
cl(L)
is the whole
space, while
\dc L
is the whole space minus
a_{\omega}
.

Remark 9.8

Conversely, not every weakly Hausdorff space is quasi-Polish. For
example,
\mathbb{Q}
with its metric topology is Hausdorff, but not
Baire, hence not quasi-Polish.

There is a
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}
endofunctor on
\mathbf{Top}
, and we will enquire
which projective limits it preserves.

Lemma 9.9

\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}
is an endofunctor on
\mathbf{Top}
, whose action on
morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
is given by

\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}f(L)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc f[L]\cap cl(f[L])
for every lens

L
on
X
; for every open subset
V
of
Y
,

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}f)}^{-1}(\Box V)=\Box{f^{-1}(V)}
and

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}f)}^{-1}(\Diamond V)=\Diamond{f^{-1}(V)}
. The
collection of maps
\iota_{X}
, when
X
ranges over all topological
spaces, is a natural transformation from
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}
to
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

Proof 26

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be any continuous map. For every lens
L
on

X
,
f[L]
is compact, so
\upc f[L]
is compact saturated, and

cl(f[L])
is clearly closed. Given any point
x\in L
,
f(x)

is in
\upc f[L]
and in
cl(f[L])
, so
\upc f[L]
intersects

cl(f[L])
, showing that
\upc f[L]\cap cl(f[L])
is a lens.

For every open subset
V
of
Y
,
{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}f)}^{-1}(\Box V)
is
the collection of lenses
L
on
X
such that

\upc f[L]\cap cl(f[L])\subseteq V
. If so, then

f[L]\subseteq\upc f[L]\cap cl(f[L])\subseteq V
, so

L\subseteq f^{-1}(V)
, namely
V\in\Box{f^{-1}(V)}
.
Conversely, if
L\subseteq f^{-1}(V)
, namely if

f[L]\subseteq V
, then
\upc f[L]\subseteq V
since
V
is
upwards-closed, so
\upc f[L]\cap cl(f[L])\subseteq V
.
Therefore
{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}f)}^{-1}(\Box V)=\Box{f^{-1}(V)}
. The
set
{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}f)}^{-1}(\Diamond V)
is the collection of lenses

L
on
X
such that
\upc f[L]\cap cl(f[L])
intersects
V
.
If so, then
cl(f[L])
intersects
V
, so
f[L]
intersects
V
,
namely
L\cap f^{-1}(V)\neq\emptyset
, or equivalently

L\in\Diamond{f^{-1}(L)}
. Conversely, if
f[L]
intersects

V
, then the larger set
\upc f[L]\cap cl(f[L])
also intersects

V
. Therefore

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}f)}^{-1}(\Diamond V)=\Diamond{f^{-1}(V)}
. All
this shows that
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}f
is continuous.

In order to show that

\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathrm{id}_{X}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}X}
, we need to show
that
L=\upc L\cap cl(L)
for every lens
L
on
X
. This is
the fact that
\varrho_{X}\circ\iota_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X}
, see
Lemma
9.2
. We also need to show that

\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{(g\circ f)}=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}g\circ\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}f
for all
continuous maps
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Z
. We
realize that the inverse image of any subbasic open set
\Box W

(resp.
\Diamond W
),
W\in{\mathcal{O}}Z
, by any side of the equality
is equal to
\Box{(f^{-1}(g^{-1}(W)))}
(resp.,

\Diamond{(f^{-1}(g^{-1}(W)))}
) Hence the inverse images of any
open subset of
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}Z
by the two sides of the equality are
the same. But any two continuous maps from a space to a
T_{0}
space
with that property are equal.

Finally, we need to verify that

\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f\circ\iota_{X}=\iota_{Y}\circ\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}f
, for every
continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
. We use the same trick. Using
the first part of Lemma
9.2
notably, the inverse
image of every subbasic open subset
\Box^{\mathrm{q}}V
(resp.,

\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}V
) of
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}Y
by each side of the equality is

\Box{f^{-1}(V)}
, resp.
\Diamond{f^{-1}(V)}
. We conclude
that the equality holds, since
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}Y
is
T_{0}
. ∎

Proposition 9.10

The comparison map
\varphi\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}X\to Z
of any
projective
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}
-situation is a topological embedding.

Proof 27

We use Lemma
3.3
, first checking that
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}

is
R
-nice with
R\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\mathsf{M},1\}
,

B_{X}(1,U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Box U
,
B_{X}(\mathsf{M},U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Diamond U
.
Property 1 of Definition
3.2
stems from the fact
that
\Box
and
\Diamond
are Scott-continuous;
\Diamond
even
commutes with arbitrary unions, and the argument for
\Box
is as in
Proposition
8.4
, considering that every lens
L

is compact: for every directed family
{(U_{i})}_{i\in I}
of open
subsets of
X
,
L\in\Box{\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}U_{i}}
if and only if

L\subseteq\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}U_{i}
, if and only if
L\subseteq U_{i}

for some
i\in I
(by compactness), if and only if

L\in\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\Box{U_{i}}
. Property 2 follows from the
characterization of
{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}f)}^{-1}
given in the first part
of Lemma
9.9
. ∎

Theorem 9.11

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. If every
X_{i}
is sober, if
\iota_{X}

is surjective, and if
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
is a projective limit of

{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
, then
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}X
is a projective limit of

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{X_{j}}\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
.

Proof 28

By Proposition
9.10
, it suffices to show that
the comparison map
\varphi\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}X\to Z
is surjective,
where
Z
is the canonical projective limit of

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{X_{j}}\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
. Let
{(L_{i})}_{i\in I}
be an
element of the latter. We form the quasi-lenses

(Q_{i},C_{i})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\iota_{X_{i}}(L_{i})
for each
i\in I
. For all

i\sqsubseteq j\in I
,
L_{i}=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{p_{ij}}(L_{j})
, so

(Q_{i},C_{i})=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{ij}}(Q_{j},C_{j})
by naturality of
\iota

(Lemma
9.9
). Using Theorem
8.8
,
there is a (unique) quasi-lens
(Q,C)
on
X
such that

(Q_{i},C_{i})=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(Q,C)
for every
i\in I
. Since we are
assuming that
\iota_{X}
is surjective, there is a lens
L
on
X

such that
(Q,C)=\iota_{X}(L)
. Then, for every
i\in I
,

\iota_{X_{i}}(L_{i})=(Q_{i},C_{i})=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(\iota_{X}(L))=\iota_{X_{i}}(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{p_{i}}(L))
, by naturality of
\iota
. Since

\iota_{X_{i}}
is injective (being a topological embedding, see
Lemma
9.2
),
L_{i}=\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{p_{i}}(L)
. ∎

One case where
\iota_{X}
is surjective, or equivalently, a
homeomorphism, where
X
is as in Theorem
9.11
,
is when
X
is weakly Hausdorff, by Lemma
9.2
. This
happens notably when every
X_{i}
is locally strongly sober, as we now
argue. The original definition of a locally strongly sober space is a
space in which the collection of limits of every convergent
ultrafilter is the closure of a unique point

[
13
, Definition VI-6.12]
. A space is locally
strongly sober if and only if it is sober, coherent, and weakly
Hausdorff
[
22
, Theorem 3.5]
, and every projective limit of
locally strongly sober spaces is locally strongly sober

[
24
, Theorem 5.1]
. We note that every Hausdorff
space, every stably locally compact space is weakly Hausdorff

[
22
, Proposition 2.2]
, and since they are all sober and
coherent, they are all locally strongly sober.

Another case where
\iota_{X}
is surjective is when
X
is
quasi-Polish, using Proposition
9.5
and
Lemma
9.2
. Now any projective limit of quasi-Polish
spaces is quasi-Polish
[
20
, Proposition 9.5]
, so we
are in this situation if every
X_{i}
is quasi-Polish. We recall that
every Polish space is quasi-Polish.

Hence, combining Theorem
9.11
with
Theorem
7.5
(or Corollary
8.11
), we
obtain the following.

Corollary 9.12

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. If every
X_{i}
is locally strongly
sober, or if every
X_{i}
is quasi-Polish, and:

1.

every
p_{ij}
is a proper map (e.g., a projection),

2.

or
I
has a countable cofinal subset and each
X_{i}
is
locally compact,

then
\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}X
is a projective limit of

{(\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{X_{j}}\to\mathop{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{\mathcal{V}}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
.

In case 2, we note that the combination of the requirements of
X_{i}

being locally compact and locally strongly sober is equivalent to
requiring that
X_{i}
be stably locally compact

[
13
, Proposition VI-6.15, Corollary VI-6.16]
.
Requiring instead that
X_{i}
be locally compact and quasi-Polish is
equivalent to requiring that
X_{i}
be locally compact, sober and
second-countable. Indeed, every quasi-Polish space is
second-countable by definition, and conversely every locally compact,
sober, second-countable space is quasi-Polish

[
5
, Theorem 44]
.

10
Subcontinuation functors

All the functors we will consider from now on are subcontinuation
functors, in a sense we define below. (All the functors we have
examined until now are naturally isomorphic to subcontinuation
functors, too, but it was easier to deal with them as we did.) We
will see that, whenever
T
is a subcontinuation functor, the
comparison maps
\varphi\colon TX\to Z
are topological embeddings,
and even homeomorphisms when
X
is obtained as a limit of an
ep-system.

For every space
X
, let
\mathcal{L}X
be the set of lower semicontinuous
maps from
X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, namely the set of continuous maps from
X

to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
where the latter is given the Scott topology of its usual
ordering.
\mathcal{L}X
is ordered pointwise, and we give it the Scott
topology of that ordering.

Definition 10.1

A
subcontinuation functor

T
is an endofunctor on
\mathbf{Top}

such that:

•

for every space
X
,
TX
is a subspace of the space
KX
of
lower semicontinuous maps from
\mathcal{L}X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, with the
topology generated by subbasic open sets

[h>r]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{F\in TX\mid F(h)>r\}
,
h\in\mathcal{L}X

(the subspace topology induced by the inclusion in the product

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{\mathcal{L}X}
);

•

for every morphism
f\colon X\to Y
,
Tf
maps every

F\in TX
to the function
h\in\mathcal{L}Y\mapsto F(h\circ f)
.

K
itself, which maps every space
X
to the space
KX
of lower
semicontinuous maps from
\mathcal{L}X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, is the largest
subcontinuation functor, which one may call the
continuation

functor. The name is by analogy with the continuation monad used in
the denotational semantics of programming languages, with answer type

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

We need the following.

Lemma 10.2

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system in
\mathbf{Top}
, with canonical projective limit

X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

1.

there is a largest function
h_{i}\in\mathcal{L}{X_{i}}
such that

h_{i}\circ p_{i}\leq h
, for every
i\in I
;

2.

for all
i\sqsubseteq j\in I
,
h_{i}\circ p_{ij}\leq h_{j}
;

3.

for all
i\sqsubseteq j\in I
,
h_{i}\circ p_{i}\leq h_{j}\circ p_{j}
;

4.

for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
h^{-1}(]r,\infty])=\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}p_{i}^{-1}(h_{i}^{-1}(]r,\infty]))
;

5.

\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}(h_{i}\circ p_{i})=h
.

Proof 29

1. Any pointwise supremum of lower semicontinuous maps is lower
semicontinuous, including the empty supremum, which is the constant
zero map. Let
\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{k\in\mathcal{L}{X_{i}}\mid k\circ p_{i}\leq h\}
, and
h_{i}
be its pointwise supremum. Then
h_{i}\in\mathcal{L}{X_{i}}
, and it is clear that
h_{i}\circ p_{i}\leq h
, so
h_{i}

is the largest element of
\mathcal{F}
.

2. We have
h_{i}\circ p_{i}=(h_{i}\circ p_{ij})\circ p_{j}\leq h
.
By the maximality of
h_{j}
,
h_{i}\circ p_{ij}\leq h_{j}
.

3. By item 2, post-composing with
p_{j}
.

4. Let
V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}h_{i}^{-1}(]r,\infty])
. Letting
\chi_{V}
be the
characteristic function of
V
,
r\chi_{V}
is a lower semicontinuous
map such that
r\chi_{V}\circ p_{i}\leq h
: indeed, for every

x\in X
, if
p_{i}(x)\in V
then
h(x)\geq h_{i}(p_{i}(x))>r
.
By the maximality of
h_{i}
,
r\chi_{V}\leq h_{i}
, which means that
every point
x
of
p_{i}^{-1}(h_{i}^{-1}(]r,\infty]))
is such that

r\chi_{V}(p_{i}(x))=r\leq h_{i}(x)
.

4. Since
h_{i}\circ p_{i}\leq h
for every
i\in I
,

h^{-1}(]r,\infty])\supseteq\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}p_{i}^{-1}(h_{i}^{-1}(]r,\infty]))
. For the reverse inclusion, let
x
be any point in

h^{-1}(]r,\infty])
. We pick
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

r<t<h(x)
, and we let
U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}h^{-1}(]t,\infty])
. We
recall that there is a largest open subset
U_{i}
of
X_{i}
such that

p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})\subseteq U
, for every
i\in I
, and that

\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})=U
. Hence
x\in p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})

for some
i\in I
. We note that
t\chi_{U_{i}}\circ p_{i}\leq h
,
since every point mapped by
p_{i}
into
U_{i}
is in

p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})\subseteq U=h^{-1}(]t,\infty])
. By maximality
of
h_{i}
,
t\chi_{U_{i}}\leq h_{i}
. Then

h_{i}(p_{i}(x))\geq t\chi_{U_{i}}(p_{i}(x))=t
, since

x\in p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})
. Since
t>r
, it follows that

x\in p_{i}^{-1}(h_{i}^{-1}(]r,\infty]))
.

5. It suffices to observe that, for every
x\in X
, for every
i\in I
, for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
r<\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}(h_{i}\circ p_{i})(x)
if and only if
x\in\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}p_{i}^{-1}(h_{i}^{-1}(]r,\infty]))
,
r<h(x)
if and only if
x\in h^{-1}(]r,\infty])
,
and to apply item 4. ∎

Lemma 10.3

Let
T
be a subcontinuation functor. Given any projective

T
-situation as given in Definition
3.1
, the
comparison map
\varphi
is a topological embedding.

Proof 30

Let
\varphi\colon TX\to Z
, where
Z,{(q_{i})}_{i\in I}
is the
canonical projective limit of the projective system

(T{p_{ij}}\colon T{X_{j}}\to T{X_{i}})_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
,
and
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
is that of

{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
.

For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
, for every
i\in I
, let

h_{i}\in\mathcal{L}{X_{i}}
be the largest such that

h_{i}\circ p_{i}\leq h
, as given in Lemma
10.2
.
For every subbasic open subset
[h>r]
of
TX
, with

h\in\mathcal{L}X
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we wish to show that
[h>r]
is
the inverse image under
\varphi
of some open subset of
Z
. We
note that for every
F\in TX
,
F\in[h>r]
if and only if

F(\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}(h_{i}\circ p_{i}))>r
(by Lemma
10.2
,
item 4), if and only if
\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}F(h_{i}\circ p_{i})>r

(since
F
is Scott-continuous) if and only if

F(h_{i}\circ p_{i})>r
for some
i\in I
; so

[h>r]=\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}[h_{i}\circ p_{i}>r]
. Now we note that

[h_{i}\circ p_{i}>r]=\varphi^{-1}(q_{i}^{-1}([h_{i}>r]))
.
Indeed,
q_{i}\circ\varphi=T{p_{i}}
, so the elements of

\varphi^{-1}(q_{i}^{-1}([h_{i}>r]))
are exactly the elements

F\in TX
such that
(q_{i}\circ\varphi)(F)\in[h_{i}>r]
, namely
such that
T{p_{i}}(F)(h_{i})>r
, equivalently such that

F(h_{i}\circ p_{i})>r
; those are exactly the elements of

[h_{i}\circ p_{i}>r]
. Hence

[h>r]=\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\varphi^{-1}(q_{i}^{-1}([h_{i}>r]))=\varphi^{-1}(\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}q_{i}^{-1}([h_{i}>r]))
, showing that

\varphi
is full.

Finally, the preorder of specialization of
TX
is given by

F\leq F^{\prime}
if and only if for all
h\in\mathcal{L}X
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

F\in[h>r]
implies
F^{\prime}\in[h>r]
, if and only if

F(h)\leq F^{\prime}(h)
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
. This is
antisymmetric, so
TX
is
T_{0}
. It follows that
\varphi
is a
topological embedding. ∎

Directed suprema, in fact arbitrary suprema, of elements of
KX
are
again in
KX
, where
K
is the continuation functor, and
X
is an
arbitrary space. This is because arbitrary suprema of lower
semicontinuous maps are lower semicontinuous. A
subdcpo
of a
dcpo
P
is a subset
A
of
P
such that the supremum of any directed
family
D\subseteq A
, taken in
P
, belongs to
A
. This entails
that
A
is itself a dcpo, but the property is strictly stronger. By
some abuse of language, we will extend this to subdcpos of
KX
,
implicitly seing the latter as a dcpo.

Proposition 10.4

Let
T
be a subcontinuation functor. Given any projective

T
-situation as given in Definition
3.1
, whose
projective system is an ep-system, and if
TX
is a subdcpo of
KX
,
then the comparison map
\varphi
is a homeomorphism.

Proof 31

We take the same notations as in Definition
3.1

(
T
-situations). Let
e_{ij}
be embeddings associated with each
of the projections
p_{ij}
. By
[
20
, Lemma 4.1]
,
each
p_{i}
is a projection, and there are associated embeddings

e_{i}\colon X_{i}\to X
, such that
e_{j}\circ e_{ij}=e_{i}
for all

i\sqsubseteq j\in I
. Moreover,

{(e_{i}(p_{i}(\vec{x})))}_{i\in I,\sqsubseteq}
is a monotone net
with supremum equal to
\vec{x}
for every
\vec{x}\in X
.

Using Lemma
10.3
, it remains to show that

\varphi
is surjective.

Let
{(F_{i})}_{i\in I}
be any element of
Z
, that is, each
F_{i}

is in
TX_{i}
and
T{p_{ij}}(F_{j})=F_{i}
for all

i\sqsubseteq j\in I
. The elements
T{e_{i}}(F_{i})\in TX
form a
monotone net, namely
T{e_{i}}(F_{i})\leq T{e_{j}}(F_{j})
for all

i\sqsubseteq j\in I
. Indeed, this follows from the fact that

e_{i}\circ p_{ij}\leq e_{j}
(because
e_{j}\circ e_{ij}=e_{i}

e_{ij}\circ p_{ij}\leq\mathrm{id}_{X_{j}}
, and
e_{i}
is continuous,
hence monotonic). Then, for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

T{e_{j}}(F_{j})(h)=F_{j}(h\circ e_{j})\geq F_{j}(h\circ e_{i}\circ p_{ij})
(since composition with the continuous map
h
is
monotonic, and
F_{j}
is continuous hence monotonic as well)

=T{p_{ij}}(F_{j})(h\circ e_{i})=F_{i}(h\circ e_{i})=T{e_{i}}(F_{i})(h)
.

Since
TX
is a subdcpo of
KX
, the monotone net

{(T{e_{j}}(F_{j}))}_{j\in I,\sqsubseteq}
has a pointwise supremum,
which is in
TX
. Let us call it
F
. We show that

\varphi(F)={(F_{i})}_{i\in I}
, or equivalently, that

T{p_{i}}(F)=F_{i}
for every
i\in I
. We consider any

h\in\mathcal{L}X
, and we aim to prove that

T{p_{i}}(F)(h)=F_{i}(h)
, namely that

F(h\circ p_{i})=F_{i}(h)
, or equivalently, that

\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{j\in J}F_{j}(h\circ p_{i}\circ e_{j})=F_{i}(h)
. By taking

j\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}i
and recalling that
p_{i}\circ e_{i}=\mathrm{id}_{X_{i}}
,
we see that the left-hand side is larger than or equal to the
right-hand side. For the other inequality, we consider any

j\in J
and we show that

F_{j}(h\circ p_{i}\circ e_{j})\leq F_{i}(h)
. Let us pick
k\in I

such that
i,j\sqsubseteq k
. Then

p_{i}\circ e_{j}\circ p_{jk}\leq p_{ik}
: indeed,

p_{i}\circ e_{j}=p_{ik}\circ p_{k}\circ e_{k}\circ e_{jk}=p_{ik}\circ e_{jk}
, so

p_{i}\circ e_{j}\circ p_{jk}\leq p_{ik}\circ e_{jk}\circ p_{jk}\leq p_{ik}
, using implicitly that continuous maps are monotonic.
Therefore
F_{j}(h\circ p_{i}\circ e_{j})
, which is equal to

T{p_{jk}}(F_{k})(h\circ p_{i}\circ e_{j})=F_{k}(h\circ p_{i}\circ e_{j}\circ p_{jk})
is less than or equal to
F_{k}(h\circ p_{ik})

(since
F_{j}
and
h
are themselves continuous hence monotonic), and
the latter is equal to
T{p_{ik}}(F_{k})(h)=F_{i}(h)
. ∎

11
Superlinear previsions and retracts

Previsions form models of mixed non-deterministic and probabilistic
choice
[
15
]
, and are an elaboration on Walley’s notion of
prevision in economics
[
52
]
. We will borrow most of
what we need from
[
19
]
, see also the errata

[
23
]
. A
prevision
on a space
X
is a
Scott-continuous map
F\colon\mathcal{L}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
that is

positively homogeneous
in the sense that
F(ah)=aF(h)
for all

a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
h\in\mathcal{L}X
. There is a space
\mathbb{P}X
of
previsions on
X
, whose topology is generated by sets

[h>r]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{F\mid F(h)>r\}
,
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

For example, any continuous valuation
\nu
on
X
gives rise to a
prevision
G\colon h\mapsto\int h\,d\nu
. Such a prevision is

linear
, in the sense that
G(h+h^{\prime})=G(h)+G(h^{\prime})
for all

h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
. Let
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}X
be the subspace of

\mathbb{P}X
of linear previsions. Conversely, every linear prevision

G\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}X
gives rise to a continuous valuation

U\mapsto G(\chi_{U})
, where
\chi_{U}
is the characteristic map of
the open set
U
, and the two constructions are inverse of each other.
Additionally, those two constructions define continuous maps between

{\mathbf{V}}X
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}X

[
49
, Satz 4.16]
. We
will therefore equate continuous valuations with linear previsions.

A prevision is
sublinear
(resp.,
superlinear
) if and
only if
G(h+h^{\prime})\leq G(h)+G(h^{\prime})
(resp.,
\geq
) for all

h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
. As in
[
19
]
, we write

\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}X
for the subspace of
\mathbb{P}X
consisting of sublinear
previsions, and
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}X
for the subspace of
\mathbb{P}X

consisting of superlinear previsions.

Among the continuous valuations, there are the probability valuations
and the subprobability valuations. Similarly, we say that a prevision

F
is
subnormalized
(resp.,
normalized
) iff

F(\mathbf{1}+h)\leq\mathbf{1}+F(h)
(resp.,
=
) for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,
where
\mathbf{1}
is the constant function with value
1
. The
homeomorphism between
{\mathbf{V}}X
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}X
restricts to
homeomorphisms between
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
(resp.,
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
) and the
subspace
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\leq 1}X
(resp.,
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{1}X
) of
subnormalized (resp., normalized) linear previsions on
X
. We write

\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\leq 1}X
,
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\leq 1}X
,

\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\leq 1}X
,
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{1}X
for the corresponding
spaces of (sub)normalized, sublinear/superlinear previsions. In
general, we write
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}X
or
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}X
,
where
\bullet
can be nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or “
1
”.

All those constructions define endofunctors on
\mathbf{Top}
, whose action

\mathbb{P}f
on morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
is given by

\mathbb{P}f(F)(h)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}F(h\circ f)
. We write
\mathbb{P}f
without
any
\bullet
superscript or any subscript
{\mathtt{P}}
,
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
or
{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}

because the action is defined in the same way for all functors. It is
easy to check that
\mathbb{P}f
is a morphism from

\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
to
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}Y
for every
continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
and similarly with
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
or
{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
in
place of
{\mathtt{P}}
. Hence all prevision functors are subcontinuation
functors in the sense of Definition
10.1
.

Additionally, this construction is compatible with the homeomorphisms

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
, meaning that those
homeomorphisms are natural. Explicitly, for every

F\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
, letting
\nu
be the associated
continuous valuation defined by
\nu(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}F(\chi_{U})
for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, for every continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
, the
continuous valuation
\nu^{\prime}
associated with
\mathbb{P}f(F)
is equal to

f[\nu]
: for every
V\in{\mathcal{O}}Y
,

\nu^{\prime}(V)=\mathbb{P}f(F)(\chi_{V})=F(\chi_{V}\circ f)=F(\chi_{f^{-1}(V)})=\nu(f^{-1}(V))=f[\nu](V)
.

Our plan for establishing projective limit preservation theorems for
prevision functors—apart from the case of ep-systems, which will
follow from Proposition
10.4
—is to rely on the fact
that spaces of previsions on
X
are retracts of

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)
, resp.
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)
under some
conditions
[
19
,
23
, Proposition 3.11, Proposition
3.22]
and to reuse our limit preservation
theorems for
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
, and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
.

A
retraction
on a category
{\mathbf{C}}
(of
Y

onto

X
) is
a pair
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
such that

r\circ s=\mathrm{id}_{X}
. (Hence an ep-pair is a special case of a
retraction.) We also say that
r
, by itself, is the retraction, with
associated
section

s
, and that
X
is a
retract
of

Y
.

We call a
natural retraction

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
of a functor

T\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
onto a functor
S\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}

any retraction in the category
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathbf{C}}
of functors from
{\mathbf{C}}

to
{\mathbf{D}}
. Explicitly, this is a collection of retractions

\textstyle{SX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{X}}
\textstyle{TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{X}}
, one for
each object
X
of
{\mathbf{C}}
, which are natural in
X
.

This will be fine for
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
, but we will need the
following refinement in the case of
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
.

Given a diagram
F\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
with a limit

X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|}
, there is a small category
{\mathbf{I}}_{*}

obtained by adjoining a fresh object
*
to
{\mathbf{I}}
, with unique
morphisms from
*
to all objects of
{\mathbf{I}}_{*}
, and there is a functor

F_{*}\colon{\mathbf{I}}_{*}\to{\mathbf{C}}
that extends
F
and such that

F_{*}(*)=X
,
F_{*}(*\to X_{i})=p_{i}
for every
i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|
.
Below, and as is customary in category theory, we write
SF
for

S\circ F
, and similarly with
TF
. We write
S_{|{\mathbf{K}}}
for the
restriction of
S
to
{\mathbf{K}}
, too.

Definition 11.1

Let
S
,
T
be two functors from a category
{\mathbf{C}}
to a category

{\mathbf{D}}
. Given a diagram
F\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
, with a limit

X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|}
, an
F
-relative natural
retraction
of
T
onto
S
is a natural retraction of

T_{|{\mathbf{K}}}
onto
S_{|{\mathbf{K}}}
, for some subcategory
{\mathbf{K}}
of

{\mathbf{C}}
that contains the image of
F_{*}
.

In other words, instead of requiring the natural retraction to exist
on the whole category
{\mathbf{D}}
, we only require it to exist on a
sufficiently large subcategory
{\mathbf{K}}
. In all cases we will
encounter,
{\mathbf{K}}
will be a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{D}}
. A subtle
point of this definition is that
{\mathbf{K}}
should contain not just the
objects
F(i)
,
i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|
, but also the limit
X
(and also,
all the required morphisms between them, which will hardly be a
problem if
{\mathbf{K}}
is a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{D}}
). For example,
consider a natural retraction
r,s
on the category of locally
compact sober spaces, and assume that each
F(i)
is locally compact
sober: this is not enough to make it an
F
-natural retraction, since
the limit itself may fail to be locally compact

[
24
, Proposition 3.4]
.

Lemma 11.2

Let
F\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
be a diagram with a limit

X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|}
, and let
S
and
T
be two functors
from
{\mathbf{C}}
to a category
{\mathbf{D}}
. If there is an
F_{*}
-relative
natural retraction

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
, and if

TX,{(Tp_{i})}_{i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|}
is a limit of
TF
, then

SX,{(Sp_{i})}_{i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|}
is a limit of
SF
.

Proof 32

It is clear that
SX,{(S{p_{i}})}_{i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|}
is a cone of

SF
. In order to show that it is universal, let

Y,{(q_{i})}_{i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|}
be another cone of
SF
. Then

Y,{(s_{F(i)}\circ q_{i})}_{i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|}
is a cone of
TF
: for
every morphism
\varphi\colon j\to i
in
{\mathbf{I}}
,

TF(\varphi)\circ s_{F(j)}\circ q_{j}=s_{F(i)}\circ SF(\varphi)\circ q_{j}=s_{F(i)}\circ q_{i}
by
F_{*}
-relative
naturality of
s
and the definition of a cone of
SF
. By
assumption,
TX,{(T{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is a limit of
TF
, so there
is a unique morphism
f\colon Y\to TX
such that

T{p_{i}}\circ f=s_{F(i)}\circ q_{i}
for every
i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|
.
Then
r_{F(i)}\circ T{p_{i}}\circ f=q_{i}
for every

i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|
, since
r_{F(i)}
and
s_{F(i)}
form a
retraction. By
F_{*}
-relative naturality of
r
,

r_{F(i)}\circ T{p_{i}}\circ f=S{p_{i}}\circ r_{X}\circ f
, so we
have found a morphism
g
such that
S{p_{i}}\circ g=q_{i}
for
every
i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|
, namely
r_{X}\circ f
. This is the only one:
given any morphism
g\colon Y\to SX
such that

S{p_{i}}\circ g=q_{i}
for every
i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|
, we must have

s_{F(i)}\circ S{p_{i}}\circ g=s_{F(i)}\circ q_{i}
for every

i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|
, namely

T{p_{i}}\circ s_{X}\circ g=s_{F(i)}\circ q_{i}
for every

i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|
, by
F_{*}
-relative naturality of
s
. By the
uniqueness of
f
,
f=s_{X}\circ g
, so
r_{X}\circ f=g
since

r_{X}\circ s_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X}
. Hence
g
is unique. ∎

By Proposition 3.22 of
[
19
]
, for every topological space

X
, and letting
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or “
1
”, there
is a retraction

r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X)\to\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}X
, defined by
r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}(Q)(h)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\min_{G\in Q}G(h)
, with
associated section
s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}
defined by

s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}(F)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{G\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X\mid G\geq F\}
. (The ordering
\leq
between previsions is the
specialization ordering, which is pointwise, and
\geq
is the
opposite ordering.) We write them
r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}
and

s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}
in order to make the dependency on
X
explicit,
reserving the notations
r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}
and
s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}
for the
families of maps
r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}
, resp.
s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}
, where
X

ranges over topological spaces.

This retraction even cuts down to a homeomorphism between

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X)
and
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X

[
19
, Theorem 4.15]
, where the former denotes the subspace
of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X)
consisting of convex compact
saturated subsets of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
. (A subset
A
of the
latter is
convex
if and only if for all
G_{1},G_{2}\in A
, for
every
r\in[0,1]
,
rG_{1}+(1-r)G_{2}\in A
.)

Lemma 11.3

The transformations
r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
are natural.

Proof 33

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be any continuous map. For
r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
, we need
to show that for every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X)
, for
every
h\in\mathcal{L}Y
,

r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f)(Q))(h)=\mathbb{P}f(r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}(Q))(h)
.
The left-hand side is equal to

\min_{G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f)(Q)}G^{\prime}(h)=\min_{G^{\prime}\in\upc\{\mathbb{P}f(G)\mid G\in Q\}}G^{\prime}(h)=\min_{G^{\prime}\in\{\mathbb{P}f(G)\mid G\in Q\}}G^{\prime}(h)=\min_{G\in Q}\mathbb{P}f(G)(h)
, while the
right-hand side is equal to

r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}(Q)(h\circ f)=\min_{G\in Q}G(h\circ f)
, and
those are equal.

For
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
, we must show that for every

F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
,

s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{P}f(F))={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f)\allowbreak(s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F))
. The left-hand side is in

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}Y)
, and we claim that so is the
right-hand side; it suffices to show that it is convex. We consider
any two elements
G^{\prime}_{1}
,
G^{\prime}_{2}
of

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f))(s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F))
, and
r\in[0,1]
. By
definition, there are elements
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
of

s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F)
such that
\mathbb{P}f(G_{1})\leq G^{\prime}_{1}
and

\mathbb{P}f(G_{2})\leq G^{\prime}_{2}
. Since
s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F)
is
convex,
rG_{1}+(1-r)G_{2}\in s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F)
. It is easy to
see that

rG^{\prime}_{1}+(1-r)G^{\prime}_{2}\geq r\mathbb{P}f(G_{1})+(1-r)\mathbb{P}g(G_{2})=\mathbb{P}f(rG_{1}+(1-r)G_{2})
, so

rG^{\prime}_{1}+(1-r)G^{\prime}_{2}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f)(s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F))
.
Since
r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}
and
r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}
are homeomorphisms (with domains

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X)
, resp.
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}Y)
), in order to show that

s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{P}f(F))={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f)(s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F))
, it suffices to show that

r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}(s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{P}f(F)))=r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f)(s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F)))
. The left-hand side is equal
to
\mathbb{P}f(F)
, and the right-hand side is equal to

\mathbb{P}f(r_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}(s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F))
(by naturality of

r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
), hence to
\mathbb{P}f(F)
. ∎

Theorem 11.4

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”. The projective
limit of a projective system

{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
of
topological spaces is preserved by
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}
if and only
if it is preserved by
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
. In particular, it is
preserved under any of the three sets of conditions of
Theorem
4.3
.

Proof 34

We start with the if direction. Let
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
be the
canonical projective limit of

{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
. If

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X,{({\mathbf{V}}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is a projective limit of

{({\mathbf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
, then

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X,{(\mathbb{P}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is a
projective limit of

{(\mathbb{P}{p_{ij}}\colon\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
. Indeed,
we recall that there is a natural homeomorphism between

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}
. The spaces

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}}
are all sober (see Remark
5.2
),
hence we can use Theorem
6.4
and conclude that

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X)},{({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{(\mathbb{P}{p_{i}})})}_{i\in I}
is a projective limit of

({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{(\mathbb{P}{p_{ij}})}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{X_{j}})}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{X_{i}})})_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
. We now use Lemma
11.2
with

S\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}
and

T\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}
, and the natural retraction

(r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}},s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})
—it is natural by
Lemma
11.3
.

In the only if direction, if

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X,{(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is a
projective limit of the projective system

(\mathbb{P}{p_{ij}}\colon\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}{X_{i}})_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
, then we claim
that the comparison map
\varphi\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\to Z
is
surjective, where
Z
is the canonical projective limit of

{({\mathbf{V}}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
. This will be enough to show
that
\varphi
is a homeomorphism, using
Proposition
4.2
. Because of the natural
homeomorphism
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}
, we reason
with linear previsions instead of continuous valuations. Let

{(G_{i})}_{i\in I}
be an element of
Z
. By assumption, there is a
(unique) superlinear prevision
F
on
X
such that

\mathbb{P}{p_{i}}(F)=G_{i}
for every
i\in I
, namely such that

F(h_{i}\circ p_{i})=G_{i}(h_{i})
for every
h_{i}\in\mathcal{L}{X_{i}}
,
for every
i\in I
. For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
, we build
h_{i}
as
in Lemma
10.2
; since
F
is Scott-continuous, we obtain
that

F(h)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}F(h_{i}\circ p_{i})=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}G_{i}(h_{i})
. Given any two maps
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
,

h+h^{\prime}=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}(h_{i}\circ p_{i})+\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}(h^{\prime}_{i}\circ p_{i})=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}(h_{i}+h^{\prime}_{i})\circ p_{i}
, so a similar
argument shows that

F(h+h^{\prime})=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}F((h_{i}+h^{\prime}_{i})\circ p_{i})=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}G_{i}(h_{i}+h^{\prime}_{i})
. Since
G_{i}
is linear, the latter is equal to

\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}(G_{i}(h_{i})+G_{i}(h^{\prime}_{i}))=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}G_{i}(h_{i})+\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}G_{i}(h^{\prime}_{i})=F(h)+F(h^{\prime})
. Hence
F
is
sublinear. Being in
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}X
, it is superlinear,
hence linear. Hence
F
is in
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
, and it was
built so that
\mathbb{P}{p_{i}}(F)=G_{i}
for every
i\in I
, so

\varphi(F)={(G_{i})}_{i\in I}
. ∎

Superlinear previsions form a model of mixed demonic non-deterministic
and probabilistic choice. Another, earlier model, due to

[
42
,
50
,
51
,
41
]
,
is the composition
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
. We have already
mentioned the fact that
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
is homeomorphic to

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
for every space
X

[
19
, Theorem 4.15]
; naturality was overlooked there, and
is dealt with by Lemma
11.3
. Together with the natural
homeomorphism
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}
, this allows
us to obtain the following.

Corollary 11.5

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”. The projective
limit of a projective system

{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
of
topological spaces is preserved by
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
if and
only if it is preserved by
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
.

We refer to Theorem
4.3
to what conditions ensure that
such limit preservation results hold.

12
Intermission:
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
preserves local compactness and
proper maps, and projective limits that yield
\odot
-consonant spaces

Before we go on with the
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}
sublinear prevision functor, we
need to prove a few theorems about the
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
functors: that
it preserves local compactness, and that it preserves proper maps. We
also need to show that certain spaces known as
\odot
-consonant
(sober) spaces are preserved by projective limits with proper bonding
maps, and that
\omega
-projective limits of locally compact sober
spaces are
\odot
-consonant.

12.1
On the preservation of local compactness by

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
