We start with local compactness. It is known that
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}

preserves various properties: stable compactness

[
3
, Theorem 39]
, being a continuous dcpo

[
31
, Theorem 5.2]
, being a quasi-continuous dcpo

[
21
, Theorem 5.1]
, for example. Some of these
preservation theorems extend over to
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}
or to
{\mathbf{V}}
, but a
conspicuously absent property in the list is local compactness. We
address this now.

The proof relies on capacities, as studied in
[
16
]
. But
that paper considers integrals of lower semicontinuous maps from
X

to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
(not
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
), hence does not cover
\mathcal{L}X
. Instead, we
will refer to
[
21
, Section 4]
, where we can find some
of the following information; we will prove the rest. For every
monotone map
\nu\colon{\mathcal{O}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, for every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
, there is a
Choquet integral

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
, defined as the indefinite Riemann
integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(h^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt
.

Lemma 12.1

The following properties hold.

1.

The Choquet integral
\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
is linear
in
\nu
, monotonic and even Scott-continuous in
\nu
.

2.

If
\nu
is Scott-continuous, then the
Choquet integral is Scott-continuous in
h
.

3.

If
\nu
is a continuous valuation, then the
Choquet integral is also linear in
h
.

4.

For every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,

\int_{x\in X}\chi_{U}(x)\,d\nu=\nu(U)
.

5.

Given any continuous valuation
\nu^{*}
on

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
, there is a Scott-continuous map

\nu\colon{\mathcal{O}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defined by

\nu(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\nu^{*}(\Box U)
for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. Then,
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
, the map

h^{*}\colon Q\mapsto\min_{x\in Q}h(x)
is in
\mathcal{L}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}

and

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}h^{*}(Q)\,d\nu^{*}
.

6.

For every compact saturated subset
Q
of

X
, the
unanimity game

\mathfrak{u}_{Q}\colon{\mathcal{O}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, which maps every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
to
1
if
Q\subseteq U
and to
0
otherwise, is
a Scott-continuous map from
{\mathcal{O}}X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

7.

Letting

\nu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\mathfrak{u}_{Q_{j}}
,
where each
Q_{j}
is compact saturated and
a_{j}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the map

F\colon\mathcal{L}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defined by:

\displaystyle F(h)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\min_{x\in Q_{j}}h(x)

for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
is a superlinear prevision.

Proof 35

1. The fact that the Choquet integral is linear in
\nu
, namely
that it commutes with scalar products by non-negative real numbers
and with addition of continuous valuations, follows from the
linearity of indefinite Riemann integration. It is also monotonic
in
\nu
. In order to show Scott-continuity, we consider a directed
family
{(\nu_{i})}_{i\in I}
, with (pointwise) supremum
\nu
, and
we observe that

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu=\int_{0}^{\infty}\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\nu_{i}(h^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt
. The key is that the integrand

t\mapsto\nu_{i}(h^{-1}(]t,\infty]))
is antitone (all antitone
maps are Riemann-integrable), and that indefinite Riemann
integration of antitone maps
f
is Scott-continuous in
f
, see

[
49
, Lemma 4.2]
. Therefore

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu_{i}(h^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu_{i}
.

2. The proof works as Tix’s original proof of the same result in
the special case where
\nu
is a continuous valuation

[
49
, Satz 4.4]
, and also relies on

[
49
, Lemma 4.2]
. Explicitly, let

{(h_{i})}_{i\in I}
be a directed family in
\mathcal{L}X
, with
(pointwise) supremum
h
. For every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

h^{-1}(]t,\infty])=\{x\in X\mid\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}h_{i}(x)>t\}=\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}h_{i}^{-1}(]t,\infty])
. Therefore

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu=\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}h_{i}^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\nu(h_{i}^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(h_{i}^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\int_{x\in X}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu
, using the Scott-continuity of
\nu
and the
Scott-continuity of indefinite Riemann integration of antitone maps.

3. This is a result of Tix
[
49
, Satz 4.4]
.

4.
\int_{x\in X}\chi_{U}(x)\,d\nu=\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(\chi_{U}^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt=\int_{0}^{1}\nu(U)\,dt+\int_{1}^{\infty}0\,dt=\nu(U)
.

5. This is as with
[
16
, Lemma 7.5]
. The fact that

\nu^{*}
is Scott-continuous follows from the fact that
\nu
is, and
that the
\Box
operator is, too. For the latter, observe that for
every directed family
{(U_{i})}_{i\in I}
of open subsets of
X
,
for every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
,
Q\in\Box{\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}U_{i}}
if and
only if
Q\subseteq\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}U_{i}
, which is equivalent to

Q\subseteq U_{i}
(namely,
Q\in\Box{U_{i}}
) for some
i\in I
,
because
Q
is compact.

For every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
, the minimum of
h(x)
when
x
ranges over

Q
is reached, since
Q
is compact and non-empty. For every

t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
Q\in{h^{*}}^{-1}(]t,\infty])
if and only if

h^{*}(Q)>t
. The latter certainly implies that
h(x)>t
for
every
x\in Q
, hence that
Q\in\Box{h^{-1}(]t,\infty])}
.
Conversely, if
Q\in\Box{h^{-1}(]t,\infty])}
, then let us pick

x\in Q
such that
h(x)
is the least value reached by
h
on

Q
; then
h^{*}(Q)=h(x)>t
, so

Q\in{h^{*}}^{-1}(]t,\infty])
. Hence we have shown that

{h^{*}}^{-1}(]t,\infty])=\Box{h^{-1}(]t,\infty])}
. This
implies that
h^{*}
is in
\mathcal{L}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
, in particular.

Now

\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}h^{*}(Q)\,d\nu^{*}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{*}({h^{*}}^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{*}(\Box h^{-1}(]t,\infty])\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(h^{-1}(]t,\infty])\,dt=\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
.

6. Monotonicity is clear. For every directed family

{(U_{i})}_{i\in I}
of open subsets of
X
,

\mathfrak{u}_{Q}(\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}U_{i})=1
if and only if

Q\in\Box{\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}U_{i}}
, which is equivalent to the
existence of an
i\in I
such that
Q\in\Box{U_{i}}

(equivalently,
\mathfrak{u}_{Q}(U_{i})=1
), as we have seen at the beginning
of the proof of item 5.

7. That would be a consequence of
[
16
, Propositions 7.2,
7.6]
, except for the fact that our functions
h
take
their values in
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. We verify that

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\mathfrak{u}_{Q_{i}}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\min_{x\in Q_{j}}h(x)
: the
first equality is by item 1, and the second one is because

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\mathfrak{u}_{Q_{i}}
is equal to

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{u}_{Q_{i}}(h^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt=\int_{0}^{\min_{x\in Q_{i}}h(x)}1\,dt+\int_{\min_{x\in Q_{i}}h(x)}^{\infty}0\,dt={\min_{x\in Q_{i}}h(x)}
.

It is easy to see that
F(h)
is superlinear, because of the laws

\min_{x\in Q_{i}}ah(x)=a\min_{x\in Q_{i}}h(x)
(for every

a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
) and

\min_{x\in Q_{i}}(h(x)+h^{\prime}(x))\geq\min_{x\in Q_{i}}h(x)+\min_{x\in Q_{i}}h^{\prime}(x)
. Scott-continuity comes from the fact that

F(h)=\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
, where

\nu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\mathfrak{u}_{Q_{j}}
, that
\nu
is
Scott-continuous (by item
6
), and by using
item
2
. ∎

As we will see,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
does not just preserve local
compactness, it maps core-compact spaces to locally compact sober
spaces. A space
X
is
core-compact
if and only if
{\mathcal{O}}X

is a continuous dcpo; every locally compact space is core-compact

[
18
, Theorem 5.2.9]
. The connection between the two
notions can be made more precise as follows. Every topological space

X
has a
sobrification

{\mathcal{S}}X
(or
X^{s}
), which is the
free sober space over
X

[
18
, Theorem 8.2.44]
; then

X
is core-compact if and only if
{\mathcal{S}}X
is locally compact

[
18
, Proposition 8.3.11]
.
{\mathcal{S}}X
can be built as
the collection of irreducible closed subsets, with the topology whose
open sets (all of them, not just a base) are

\diamond U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{F\in{\mathcal{S}}X\mid F\cap U\neq\emptyset\}
,

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. In particular,

\diamond\colon U\mapsto\diamond U
is an order-isomorphism between

{\mathcal{O}}X
and
{\mathcal{O}}{{\mathcal{S}}X}
. This induces a homeomorphism
between
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{S}}X}
.

Theorem 12.2

For every core-compact space
X
,
{\mathbf{V}}X
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
are
locally compact and sober. If
X
is also compact, then
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X

is locally compact sober.

Proof 36

By Remark
5.2
, all the spaces
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
are
sober.

Replacing
X
by
{\mathcal{S}}X
if necessary, we may assume that
X
is
locally compact and sober. Then the upper Vietoris topology on

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
coincides with the Scott topology on
{\mathcal{Q}}X
(with the
reverse inclusion ordering
\supseteq
), by Lemma 8.3.26 of

[
18
]
, and
{\mathcal{Q}}X
itself is a continuous dcpo

[
18
, Proposition 8.3.25]
. A fundamental theorem due
to Jones
[
31
, Theorem 5.2]
states that for every
continuous dcpo
P
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}P
is a continuous dcpo under
the stochastic ordering, and that a basis is given by the simple
valuations, namely those of the form

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
, where each
a_{i}
is in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and

x_{i}\in P
. A similar result holds for
{\mathbf{V}}P

[
13
, Theorem
IV-9.16]
, and for
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}P
provided that
P
is
also pointed
[
9
, Corollary 3.3]
. We will apply those
results to
P\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathcal{Q}}X
, and we notice that if
X
is
compact, then
P
is pointed, as
X
itself will be the least
element of
P
in that case.

Let
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, and let
\mathcal{U}
be any open
neighborhood of
\nu
. Then
\nu
is in some finite intersection of
subbasic open sets
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[U_{i}>r_{i}]
that is included in

\mathcal{U}
, where each
U_{i}
is open in
X
and
r_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We
consider

\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathbf{V}}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}(\nu)\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
.
We recall that
\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}
is the unit of the
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
monad, and
maps every point
x\in X
to
\upc x\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
. For every open
subset
U
of
X
,

\mu(\Box U)=\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}[\nu](\Box U)=\nu((\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X})^{-1}(\Box U))=\nu(U)
. It follows that
\mu

is in
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[\Box{U_{i}}>r_{i}]
. The latter is open in
the upper Vietoris topology on

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}={\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}P
, hence in the Scott topology
of the stochastic ordering. Since
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}P
is a continuous
dcpo with a basis of simple valuations, there is a simple valuation

\xi^{*}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\delta_{Q_{j}}
in
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}P

that is way below
\mu
and in
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[\Box{U_{i}}>r_{i}]
.

We build a superlinear prevision
F
on
X
by letting
F(h)
be
equal to
\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\min_{x\in Q_{j}}h(x)
for every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
. (See Lemma
12.1
, item
7
.)
Equivalently,
F(h)=\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\xi
, where

\xi\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\mathfrak{u}_{Q_{j}}
. The notations
\xi
,

\xi^{*}
are justified by the fact that for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,

\xi(U)=\xi^{*}(\Box U)
.

Then
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
is a compact saturated subset of

\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
, as we have seen in
Section
11
. Equating
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X

with
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
,
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
is the subset of those

\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

F(h)\leq\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu^{\prime}
. We claim that
\nu
is
in the interior of
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
, and that
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)

is included in
\mathcal{U}
; this will end our proof.

We start by showing that
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)\subseteq\mathcal{U}
.
Let
\nu^{\prime}
be any element of
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
. In other words,
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu^{\prime}\geq F(h)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\min_{x\in Q_{j}}h(x)
. For each
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, we apply the
latter to
h\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\chi_{U_{i}}
. We realize that

\int_{x\in X}\chi_{U_{i}}(x)\,d\nu^{\prime}=\nu^{\prime}(U_{i})

(Lemma
12.1
, item
4
), and that

\min_{Q_{j}}\chi_{U_{i}}
is equal to
1
if
Q_{j}\subseteq U_{i}
, to

0
otherwise, so

F(\chi_{U_{i}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq m\\
Q_{j}\subseteq U_{i}\end{subarray}}a_{j}
. Therefore

\nu^{\prime}(U_{i})\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq m\\
Q_{j}\subseteq U_{i}\end{subarray}}a_{j}
. We recall that
\xi^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\delta_{Q_{j}}
is in

\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[\Box{U_{i}}>r_{i}]
, so for every

i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
,
\xi^{*}(\Box{U_{i}})>r_{i}
, namely

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq m\\
Q_{j}\subseteq U_{i}\end{subarray}}a_{j}>r_{i}
.
Hence
\nu^{\prime}\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[U_{i}>r_{i}]\subseteq\mathcal{U}
.

Next, we verify that
\nu
is in the interior of

s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
. We use the fact that
\xi^{*}
is way below

\mu
, equivalently that
\mu
is in the open set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}\xi^{*}
.
Since
\mu={\mathbf{V}}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}(\nu)
,
\nu
is in

({\mathbf{V}}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}})^{-1}(\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}\xi^{*})
, which is open since

{\mathbf{V}}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}
is continuous. It remains to show that

({\mathbf{V}}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}})^{-1}(\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}\xi^{*})
is included in

s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
.

For every
\nu^{\prime}\in({\mathbf{V}}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}})^{-1}(\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}\xi^{*})
,
by definition
\xi^{*}
is way below, in particular below

\mu^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathbf{V}}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}(\nu^{\prime})
. The latter is such that

\mu^{\prime}(\Box U)=\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}[\nu^{\prime}](\Box U)=\nu^{\prime}((\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X})^{-1}(\Box U))=\nu^{\prime}(U)
for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. Hence we may write
\mu^{\prime}
as
{\nu^{\prime}}^{*}
and apply
Lemma
12.1
, item
5
, so that

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu^{\prime}=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)\,d{\nu^{\prime}}^{*}
. Since
\xi^{*}\leq\mu^{\prime}={\nu^{\prime}}^{*}
, the latter is
larger than or equal to

\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)\,d\xi^{*}=\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\xi=F(h)
. (We use Lemma
12.1
,
item
5
on the pair
\xi
,
\xi^{*}
for that.) We have
shown that
\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu^{\prime}\geq F(h)
for every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
, so
\nu^{\prime}\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
, as promised.
∎

12.2
Proper maps and quasi-adjoints

In order to see that
{\mathbf{V}}
preserves proper maps, we rely on the
following notion, a very close cousin of the quasi-retractions of

[
17
, Section 4]
, which were used to characterize proper
surjective maps there. We recall that

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\colon X\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
is the unit of the
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}
monad,
and that it maps every
x\in X
to
\upc x
. We also recall that the
specialization ordering on spaces of the form
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
is

reverse
inclusion
\supseteq
.

Definition 12.3

A
quasi-adjoint
to a continuous map
r\colon X\to Y
is a
continuous map
\varsigma\colon Y\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
such that:

(a)

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{Y}\leq{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}r\circ\varsigma
, namely

\upc y\supseteq{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}r(\varsigma(y))
for every

y\in Y
, and

(b)

\varsigma\circ r\leq\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}
, namely

x\in\varsigma(r(x))
for every
x\in X
.

Lemma 12.4

For a continuous map
r\colon X\to Y
, the following are
equivalent:

1.

r
is a proper map;

2.

\dc r[F]
is closed for every closed subset
F
of
X
and

r^{-1}(\upc y)
is compact for every
y\in Y
;

3.

r
has a quasi-adjoint.

The quasi-adjoint
\varsigma
, if it exists, is uniquely determined
by
\varsigma(y)=r^{-1}(\upc y)
for every
y\in Y
.

Proof 37

The equivalence between items 1 and 2 can be found in

[
13
, Lemma VI-6.21]
.

We make the following observation:
(*)
for every map

\varsigma\colon Y\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
such that

\varsigma(y)=r^{-1}(\upc y)
for every
y\in Y
, for every
open subset
U
of
X
, the complement of
\varsigma^{-1}(\Box U)

in
Y
is equal to
\dc r[F]
, where
F
is the complement of
U
.
Indeed, for every
y\in Y
,
y\not\in\varsigma^{-1}(\Box U)
if
and only if
\varsigma(y)=r^{-1}(\upc y)
is not included in

U
, if and only if there is an
x\in F
such that
y\leq r(x)
.

3\mathrel{\Rightarrow}2
. Let
\varsigma
be a quasi-adjoint of
r
. We claim
that
\varsigma(y)=r^{-1}(\upc y)
, which will also show the
final uniqueness result. For every
x\in\varsigma(y)
,
r(x)

is in
\upc r[\varsigma(y)]\subseteq\upc y
(by (a)), so

y\leq r(x)
, namely
x\in r^{-1}(\upc y)
. Conversely, for
every
x\in r^{-1}(\upc y)
, we have
y\leq r(x)
. Since

\varsigma
is continuous hence monotonic,

\varsigma(y)\supseteq\varsigma(r(x)
. By (b),

x\in\varsigma(r(x))
, so
x\in\varsigma(y)
.

It follows that, since
\varsigma(y)\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
by
assumption,
r^{-1}(\upc y)
is compact. For every closed subset

F
of
X
, we consider its complement
U
. Since
\varsigma
is
continuous,
\varsigma^{-1}(\Box U)
is open. But its complement
is precisely
\dc r[F]
, by
(*)
, so
\dc r[F]
is closed.

1\mathrel{\Rightarrow}3
. Let
\varsigma(y)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}r^{-1}(\upc y)
for every

y\in Y
. This is compact saturated since
r
is proper, hence
perfect. Hence
\varsigma
is a map from
Y
to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
.

We check that
\varsigma
is continuous. For every open subset
U

of
X
,
\varsigma^{-1}(\Box U)
is the complement of
\dc r[F]
,
where
F\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}X\smallsetminus U
, by
(*)
. Since
r
is proper,

\dc r[F]
is closed, so
\varsigma^{-1}(\Box U)
is open.

Let us check (a). For every
y\in Y
,

({\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}r\circ\varsigma)(y)=\upc r[\varsigma(y)]=\upc r[r^{-1}(\upc y)]
. Every element
y^{\prime}
of that set is such that

y^{\prime}\geq r(x)
for some
x\in X
such that
r(x)\geq y
, so

y^{\prime}\in\upc y
.

Let us check (b). For every
x\in X
, we need to check that

x\in\varsigma(r(x))=r^{-1}(\upc r(x))
, or equivalently
that
r(x)\geq r(x)
, which is obvious. ∎

12.3
On the preservation of proper maps by
{\mathbf{V}}

Lemma 12.5

For every topological space
X
, there is a continuous map

\Phi\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
defined
by

\Phi(\mu)(h)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)\,d\mu
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
.

Proof 38

Given
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
, we may define

\nu^{*}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu
and
\nu(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\nu^{*}(\Box U)
for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. Then
\nu
is Scott-continuous and

\Phi(\mu)(h)=\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
by
Lemma
12.1
, item
5
, so
\Phi(\mu)
is
Scott-continuous in
h
by Lemma
12.1
,
item
2
.

We claim that
\Phi(\mu)
is positively homogeneous. We write

h^{*}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
for the map

Q\mapsto\min_{x\in Q}h(x)
. This is in
\mathcal{L}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
, by
Lemma
12.1
, item
5
.

For every
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
(ah)^{*}=ah^{*}
. Therefore

\Phi(\mu)(ah)=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(ah)^{*}(Q)\,d\mu=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}ah^{*}(Q)\,d\mu=a\Phi(\mu)(h)
, by linearity of
integration (Lemma
12.1
, item
3
).

We claim that
\Phi(\mu)
is superlinear. Let

h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
. Then

\Phi(\mu)(h+h^{\prime})=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}(h(x)+h^{\prime}(x))\,d\mu
. For every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
,

\min_{x\in Q}(h(x)+h^{\prime}(x))\geq\min_{x\in Q}h(x)+\min_{x\in Q^{\prime}}h^{\prime}(x)
. Since
\mu
is a continuous valuation,
integration with respect to
\mu
is linear
(Lemma
12.1
, item
3
), and monotonic (as a consequence of
Lemma
12.1
, item
2
) in the integrated function, so

\Phi(\mu)(h+h^{\prime})\geq\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)\,d\mu+\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h^{\prime}(x)\,d\mu=\Phi(\mu)(h)+\Phi(\mu)(h^{\prime})
.

Hence
\Phi(\mu)
is a superlinear prevision. We note that

\min_{x\in Q}(1+h(x))=1+\min_{x\in Q}h(x)
, for every
non-empty compact saturated subset of
X
and for every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
. If
\mu(X)\leq 1
, then for every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\Phi(\mu)(\mathbf{1}+h)=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}(1+h(x))\,d\mu=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}1\,d\mu+\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)\,d\mu\leq 1+\Phi(\mu)(h)
.
Similarly, if
\mu(X)=1
, then

\Phi(\mu)(\mathbf{1}+h)=1+\Phi(\mu)(h)
. Therefore
\Psi
is
a map from
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
to
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
.

It remains to show that
\Phi
is continuous. For every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
, for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\Phi^{-1}([h>r])=[h^{*}>r]
, where

h^{*}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is defined by

h^{*}(Q)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)
, as above. Note that
h^{*}
is
in
\mathcal{L}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
, by Lemma
12.1
, item
5
.
∎

Corollary 12.6

For every topological space
X
, there is a continuous map from

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
, which
maps every
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
to the collection of
continuous valuations
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that

\nu(U)\geq\mu(\Box U)
for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
.

Proof 39

We equate
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
with
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
. Then the
map
s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}\circ\Phi
is continuous, and maps every

\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
to the collection

\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mid\forall h\in\mathcal{L}X,\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)\,d\mu\leq\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\}
. Let
\xi\colon{\mathcal{O}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be defined by

\xi(\Box U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu(U)
for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, so that we
may write
\mu
as
\xi^{*}
, following the convention of
Lemma
12.1
, item
5
. By this item, for
every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
,

\nu\in(s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}\circ\Phi)(\mu)
if and only if

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\xi\leq\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
. By
taking
h\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\chi_{U}
for an arbitrary open subset
U
of
X
,
the latter implies
\xi(U)\leq\nu(U)
. Conversely, if

\xi(U)\leq\nu(U)
for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\xi=\int_{0}^{\infty}\xi(h^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(h^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt=\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
. Hence

(s_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}\circ\Phi)(\mu)
is exactly

\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mid\forall U\in{\mathcal{O}}X,\nu(U)\geq\mu(\Box U)\}
. ∎

In order to apply the theory of quasi-adjoints, we need to replace

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
by
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}
in the result above. We will do this by using the
following trick.

For any topological space
X
, let
X^{\top}
be the space obtained from

X
by adding a fresh element
\top
, and whose non-empty open subsets
are the sets
U\cup\{\top\}
,
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. The specialization
preordering
\leq^{\top}
of
X^{\top}
is such that
x\leq^{\top}y
if
and only if
y=\top
or
x,y\in X
and
x\leq y
in
X
.

As we had said in Section
6
, we will reserve
the notation
\Box U
for
\{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\mid Q\subseteq U\}
, and
use the notation
\Box_{0}U
for
\{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X\mid Q\subseteq U\}
.
Hence
\Box_{0}U=\Box U\cup\{\emptyset\}
, for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
.

Lemma 12.7

For every topological space
X
, the map

t\colon Q\mapsto Q\cup\{\top\}
is a homeomorphism of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X

onto
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
.

Proof 40

For every compact saturated subset
Q
of
X
,
Q\cup\{\top\}
is
certainly saturated and non-empty. Any open cover of

Q\cup\{\top\}
can be trimmed to one that does not contain the
empty set, hence one of the form
{(U_{i}\cup\{\top\})}_{i\in I}

with each
U_{i}
open in
X
; then
{(U_{i})}_{i\in I}
is an open
cover of
Q
, from which we can extract a finite subcover. This
shows that
Q\cup\{\top\}
is compact in
X^{\top}
.

For every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,

t^{-1}(\Box_{0}{(U\cup\{\top\})})=\Box U
, so
t
is full and
continuous. Since
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
is
T_{0}
,
t
is a topological
embedding.

It remains to show that
t
is surjective. Given any non-empty
compact saturated subset
Q^{\prime}
of
X^{\top}
,
Q^{\prime}
must contain some
point, which is below
\top
, so
Q^{\prime}
must also contain
\top
. But

\{\top\}
is open in
X^{\top}
, so its complement
X
is closed in

X^{\top}
, and therefore
Q\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}Q^{\prime}\cap X
is compact. Since it
is included in the subspace
X
,
Q
is compact in
X
, too. It is
clearly saturated in
X
, and
Q^{\prime}=Q\cup\{\top\}
, so
t
is
surjective. ∎

Lemma 12.8

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”. For every
topological space
X
, let
\mathcal{F}_{X}
be the subset of

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}
consisting of those elements
\nu
such that

\nu(\{\top\})=0
.
\mathcal{F}_{X}
is a closed subspace of

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}
, and there is a continuous map

c\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{F}_{X}}

defined by
c(\mathcal{Q})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{F}_{X}
for
every
\mathcal{Q}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}}
.

Proof 41

First, the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{X}
makes sense, and notably the
condition
\nu(\{\top\})=0
, because
\{\top\}
is open in

X^{\top}
. Second,
\mathcal{F}_{X}
is the complement of

[\{\top\}>0]
, hence is closed in
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}
.

For every
\mathcal{Q}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}}
,

c(\mathcal{Q})
is compact in
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}
and
included in
\mathcal{F}_{X}
, hence compact in
\mathcal{F}_{X}
seen as
a subspace. The specialization ordering of
\mathcal{F}_{X}
is the
restriction of the stochastic ordering, so
c(\mathcal{Q})
is
saturated in
\mathcal{F}_{X}
: it suffices to show that for all

\nu\in\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{F}_{X}
and
\nu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{X}

such that
\nu\leq\nu^{\prime}
,
\nu^{\prime}
is in
\mathcal{Q}
, which follows
from the fact that
\mathcal{Q}
is saturated in

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}
. Hence
c
defines a map from

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{F}_{X}}
. For every
open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}
,

c^{-1}(\Box_{0}{(\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{F}_{X})})=\{\mathcal{Q}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}}\mid\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{F}_{X}\subseteq\mathcal{U}\}=\Box{([\{\top\}>0]\cup\mathcal{U})}
;
indeed,
\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{F}_{X}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
if and
only if
\mathcal{Q}
is included in the union of the complement

[\{\top\}>0]
of
\mathcal{F}_{X}
with
\mathcal{U}
. Hence
c
is
continuous. ∎

Lemma 12.9

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”. For every
topological space
X
, let
\mathcal{F}_{X}
be as in
Lemma
12.8
. For every
\nu\in\mathcal{F}_{X}
, there is
a unique
\nu^{-}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that
i[\nu^{-}]=\nu
,
where
i
is the inclusion map from
X
into
X^{\top}
. The map

\_^{-}\colon\nu\mapsto\nu^{-}
is continuous from
\mathcal{F}_{X}
to

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
.

Proof 42

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{F}_{X}
. If
\nu^{-}
exists, then for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, we must have

\nu(U\cup\{\top\})=\nu^{-}(i^{-1}(U\cup\{\top\})=\nu^{-}(U)
, showing uniqueness. As far as existence is concerned, we
define
\nu^{-}(U)
as
\nu(U\cup\{\top\})
for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. This is a strict map precisely because

\nu\in\mathcal{F}_{X}
, and it is clear that
\nu^{-}
is modular and
Scott-continuous. Additionally,
\nu^{-}(X)\leq 1
if and only if

\nu(X^{\top})\leq 1
, and similarly with
=
instead of
\leq
.

This defines a map
\_^{-}\colon\nu\mapsto\nu^{-}
from

\mathcal{F}_{X}
to
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, and it remains to see that it is
continuous: the inverse image of a subbasic open set
[U>r]
, with

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, is
[U\cup\{\top\}>r]
. ∎

We recall that
\Box_{0}U
denotes
\Box U\cup\{\emptyset\}
, and is
a canonical subbasic open subset of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
, where
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
.

Proposition 12.10

For every topological space
X
, there is a continuous map from

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
, which maps every

\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X}
to the collection of continuous
valuations
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that

\nu(U)\geq\mu(\Box_{0}U)

for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
.

Proof 43

Let us call
f
the continuous map from

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{(X^{\top})}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}}

that we obtain from Corollary
12.6
applied to the space

X^{\top}
. We form the composition:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 22.64583pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.64583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.29305pt\raise 5.9014pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.9014pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}t}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.64583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.64583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{(X^{\top})}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 107.19267pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 126.47917pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 126.47917pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X^{\top}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 178.99908pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{c}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 198.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 198.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{F}_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 235.9099pt\raise 6.2853pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.8853pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{\_^{-}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 259.59523pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 259.59523pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

where
t
is from Lemma
12.7
,
c
is from
Lemma
12.8
, and
\_^{-}
is from Lemma
12.9
.
This composition is continuous.

Given any
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X}
, let
Q
be its image by
that composition. It remains to show that

Q=\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mid\forall U\in{\mathcal{O}}X,\nu(U)\geq\mu(\Box_{0}U)\}
. The image of
\mu
by
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}t
is
the continuous valuation

\mathcal{U}\mapsto\mu(t^{-1}(\mathcal{U}))=\mu(\{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X\mid Q\cup\{\top\}\in\mathcal{U}\})
. In particular, for every
open subset
U
of
X
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}t(\mu)(\Box{(U\cup\{\top\})})=\mu(\{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X\mid Q\cup\{\top\}\subseteq U\cup\{\top\}\})=\mu(\Box_{0}U)
, while
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}t(\mu)(\emptyset)=0
. The image of

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}t(\mu)
by
f
is the collection of all

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}
such that for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,

\nu(U\cup\{\top\})\geq{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}t(\mu)(\Box{(U\cup\{\top\})})
(and

\nu(\emptyset)\geq{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}t(\mu)(\emptyset)
, which is
automatically true); in other words, the collection of all

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}
such that for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,
\nu(U\cup\{\top\})\geq\mu(\Box_{0}U)
. This
is mapped by
c
to the collection
\mathcal{N}
of all

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}
satisfying the same condition and
such that
\nu(\{\top\})=0
. For any
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
, we
have
\nu^{-}(U)=\nu(U\cup\{\top\})\geq\mu(\Box_{0}U)
for
every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
.

The set
Q
is the upward closure of the collection of continuous
valuations
\nu^{-}
obtained this way. In particular, for every

\nu^{\prime}\in Q
, for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,
\nu^{\prime}(U)
is larger than
or equal to
\nu^{-}(U)
for some
\nu\in\mathcal{N}
, and therefore

\nu^{\prime}(U)\geq\mu(\Box_{0}U)
. Conversely, for every

\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that
\nu^{\prime}(U)\geq\mu(\Box_{0}U)

for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, let
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}
be
defined by
\nu(U\cup\{\top\})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\nu^{\prime}(U)
for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, and
\nu(\emptyset)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
. It is easy to
check that
\nu
is indeed in
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{(X^{\top})}
, and that

\nu(\{\top\})=0
, so that
\nu\in\mathcal{F}_{X}
. Additionally,

\nu^{-}=\nu^{\prime}
. Therefore
\nu^{\prime}
is in
Q
. Hence
Q
coincides
with the collection

\{\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mid\forall U\in{\mathcal{O}}X,\nu^{\prime}(U)\geq\mu(\Box_{0}U)\}
, as promised. ∎

The following somehow generalizes Theorem 6.5 of
[
17
]
, which
states that
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}
preserves proper surjective maps between stably
compact spaces. We do not deal with surjectivity.

Theorem 12.11

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”.
For every proper map
r\colon X\to Y
,
{\mathbf{V}}r\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Y
is proper.

Proof 44

By Lemma
12.4
,
r
has a quasi-adjoint

\varsigma\colon Y\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
. Let
\varsigma^{\prime}
be the
composition

\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{V}}\varsigma}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}
, where
g
is the
continuous map of Proposition
12.10
. We check that

\varsigma^{\prime}
is a quasi-adjoint to
{\mathbf{V}}r
.

First, we claim that

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Y}\leq{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}r}\circ\varsigma^{\prime}
,
namely that for every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Y
,

\upc\nu\supseteq{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}r}(g({\mathbf{V}}\varsigma(\nu)))
. For
every

\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}r}(g({\mathbf{V}}\varsigma(\nu)))=\upc{\mathbf{V}}r[g(\varsigma[\nu])]
, there is a
\nu^{\prime}\in g(\varsigma[\nu])
such
that
\mu^{\prime}\geq r[\nu^{\prime}]
. By definition of
g
, for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,

\nu^{\prime}(U)\geq\varsigma[\nu](\Box_{0}U)=\nu(\varsigma^{-1}(\Box_{0}U))
. Therefore, for every
V\in{\mathcal{O}}Y
,

\mu^{\prime}(V)\geq r[\nu^{\prime}](V)=\nu^{\prime}(r^{-1}(V))\geq\nu(\varsigma^{-1}(\Box_{0}{r^{-1}(V)}))
. We now observe that

V\subseteq\varsigma^{-1}(\Box_{0}{r^{-1}(V)})
: for every

y\in V
,
\upc y
is included in
V
, and since

\upc y\supseteq{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}r(\varsigma(y))
, we have

r[\varsigma(y)]\subseteq V
; hence

\varsigma(y)\in\Box_{0}{r^{-1}(V)}
. Since
\nu
is monotonic, we
conclude that
\mu^{\prime}(V)\geq\nu(V)
. Since
V
is arbitrary in

{\mathcal{O}}Y
,
\mu^{\prime}\geq\nu
. This shows that
\mu^{\prime}\in\upc\nu
.
Since
\mu^{\prime}
is arbitrary in

{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}r}(g({\mathbf{V}}\varsigma(\nu)))
, we have shown that

\upc\nu\supseteq{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}r}(g({\mathbf{V}}\varsigma(\nu)))
.

Second, we claim that
\nu\in\varsigma^{\prime}({\mathbf{V}}r(\nu))
for every

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
. We have

\varsigma^{\prime}({\mathbf{V}}r(\nu))=g({\mathbf{V}}(\varsigma\circ r))(\nu))
,
and the claim reduces to showing that

\nu(U)\geq{\mathbf{V}}(\varsigma\circ r)(\nu)(\Box_{0}U)
for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. We compute:

{\mathbf{V}}(\varsigma\circ r)(\nu)(\Box_{0}U)=(\varsigma\circ r)[\nu](\Box_{0}(U))=\nu((\varsigma\circ r)^{-1}(\Box_{0}U))
.
But
(\varsigma\circ r)^{-1}(\Box_{0}U)\subseteq U
: for every

x\in(\varsigma\circ r)^{-1}(\Box_{0}U)
,

(\varsigma\circ r)(x)\subseteq U
, and we conclude since

x\in\varsigma(r(x))
. Since
\nu
is monotonic,

{\mathbf{V}}(\varsigma\circ r)(\nu)(\Box_{0}U)\leq\nu(U)
, which is
what we wanted to prove.

We now know that
\varsigma^{\prime}
is a quasi-adjoint to
{\mathbf{V}}r
, so

{\mathbf{V}}r
is proper by Lemma
12.4
. ∎

12.4
Projective systems consisting of proper maps

Proposition 12.12

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system in
\mathbf{Top}
, with canonical projective limit

X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. Let us also assume that each
X_{j}
is sober
and that each
p_{ij}
is proper. Then:

1.

every
p_{i}
is proper; we write
\varsigma_{i}
for its quasi-adjoint;

2.

for every
i\in I
, for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, the largest
open subset
U_{i}
of
X_{i}
such that
p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})\subseteq U

is
\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}U)
;

3.

for every
i\in I
, for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
, the largest
function
h_{i}\in\mathcal{L}{X_{i}}
such that
h_{i}\circ p_{i}\leq h

is
h^{\dagger}\circ\varsigma_{i}
, where

h^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{L}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X}
maps every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
to

\min_{x\in Q}h(x)
if
Q\neq\emptyset
and
\emptyset
to

\infty
.

Proof 45

We will need to know the following. A
well-filtered space

Z
is a topological space such that for every filtered family

{(Q_{j})}_{j\in J}
of compact saturated subsets, for every open
subset
U
of
Z
, if
\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{j\in J}Q_{j}\subseteq U
then

Q_{j}\subseteq U
for some
j\in J
. It follows that for every
filtered family as above,
\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{j\in J}Q_{j}
is compact saturated

[
18
, Proposition 8.3.6]
. Every sober space is
well-filtered
[
18
, Proposition 8.3.5]
.

1. We fix
i\in I
. Using Lemma
12.4
, we will build
a quasi-adjoint
\varsigma_{i}
to
p_{i}
. We know that, for every

y\in X_{i}
,
\varsigma_{i}(y)
must be equal to
p_{i}^{-1}(\upc y)
,
but we will define it differently, so as to make sure that it is
compact saturated, and we will then check that it is equal to

p_{i}^{-1}(\upc y)
.

Let
y\in X_{i}
. For every
k\in I
such that
i\sqsubseteq k
,

p_{ik}^{-1}(\upc y)
is compact (and saturated) since
p_{ik}
is
proper. For every
j\sqsubseteq k
, we let

Q_{jk}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc p_{jk}[p_{ik}^{-1}(\upc y)]
. We claim that
for all
j,k,k^{\prime}\in I
such that

i,j\sqsubseteq k\sqsubseteq k^{\prime}
,
Q_{jk^{\prime}}\subseteq Q_{jk}
.
For every
x\in Q_{jk^{\prime}}
, there is a point
x^{\prime}\in X_{k^{\prime}}
such
that
p_{jk^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})\leq x
and
y\leq p_{ik^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})
. In other
words,
p_{jk}(p_{kk^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}))\leq x
and

y\leq p_{ik}(p_{kk^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}))
, showing that

x\in\upc p_{jk}[p_{ik}^{-1}(\upc y)]=Q_{jk}
. Hence the
family
{(Q_{jk})}_{k\in\upc i\cap\upc j}
is filtered. (We
write
\upc i
for the collection of indices
k\in I
such that

i\sqsubseteq k
, and similarly for
\upc j
. Both
\upc i
and

\upc j
, as well as their intersection, are cofinal in
I
, and in
particular directed.) Since
X_{j}
is sober hence well-filtered,

Q_{j}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{k\in\upc i\cap\upc j}Q_{jk}
is therefore a
compact saturated subset of
X_{j}
.

We verify that for all
j\sqsubseteq j^{\prime}\in I
,
p_{jj^{\prime}}
maps

Q_{j^{\prime}}
to
Q_{j}
. It suffices to verify that it maps
Q_{j^{\prime}k}
to

Q_{jk}
for every
k\in\upc i\cap\upc j^{\prime}
. For every

x\in Q_{j^{\prime}k}
, by definition there is a point
x^{\prime}\in X_{k}
such
that
p_{j^{\prime}k}(x^{\prime})\leq x
and
y\leq p_{ik}(x^{\prime})
. Then

p_{jj^{\prime}}(x)\geq p_{jj^{\prime}}(p_{j^{\prime}k}(x^{\prime}))=p_{jk}(x^{\prime})
, and

y\leq p_{ik}(x^{\prime})
, so

p_{jj^{\prime}}(x)\in\upc p_{jk}[p_{ik}^{-1}(\upc y)]=Q_{jk}
.

Hence

{(p_{jk|Q_{k}}\colon Q_{k}\to Q_{j}))}_{j\sqsubseteq k\in\upc i}
is
a projective system of compact spaces, obtained from compact
saturated subsets
Q_{i}
of each
X_{i}
. Each
X_{i}
is sober, hence
also every
Q_{i}
is sober by Remark
5.2
. By
Steenrod’s theorem, its canonical projective limit
Q
is compact.
One can verify that
Q
is in fact a compact saturated subset of
X

[
24
, Lemma 4.3]
.

We claim that
Q=p_{i}^{-1}(\upc y)
. By construction,
Q
is the
collection of tuples
\vec{x}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(x_{i})}_{i\in I}
where each

x_{j}\in Q_{j}
and for all
j\sqsubseteq k\in I
,

x_{j}=p_{jk}(x_{j})
. For each such tuple,
p_{i}(\vec{x})=x_{i}
is
in
Q_{i}
, and

Q_{i}\subseteq Q_{ii}=\upc p_{ii}[p_{ii}^{-1}(\upc y)]=\upc y
. Therefore
Q\subseteq p_{i}^{-1}(\upc y)
. Conversely, let

\vec{x}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(x_{i})}_{i\in I}
be any element of
X
such that

p_{i}(\vec{x})=x_{i}\in\upc y
. We claim that
\vec{x}
in
Q
,
namely that for every
j\in I
,
x_{j}\in Q_{j}
. In turn, we need to
show that for every
k\in\upc i\cap\upc j
,

x_{j}\in\upc p_{jk}[p_{ik}^{-1}(\upc y)]
. We simply observe
that
x_{j}=p_{jk}(x_{k})
(hence in particular

x_{j}\geq p_{jk}(x_{j})
) and
x_{k}\in p_{ik}^{-1}(\upc y)
, since

p_{ik}(x_{k})=x_{i}\in\upc y
, by assumption.

Using Lemma
12.4
, let
\varsigma_{jk}
be the
quasi-adjoint of
p_{jk}
, for all
j\sqsubseteq k\in I
. We know
that
\varsigma_{jk}(x)=p_{jk}^{-1}(\upc x)
for every

x\in X_{j}
. Hence
Q_{jk}
, as defined above, is equal to

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{jk}}(\varsigma_{ik}(y))
, and

Q_{j}=\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{k\in\upc i\cap\upc j}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{jk}}(\varsigma_{ik}(y))
.

For every
y\in X_{i}
, let us define
\varsigma_{i}(y)
as

p_{i}^{-1}(\upc y)
, namely as the intersection

X\cap\prod_{j\in I}\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{k\in\upc i\cap\upc j}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{jk}}(\varsigma_{ik}(y))
. We claim that
\varsigma_{i}
is
continuous. It suffices to show that the inverse image of a basic
open set
\Box_{0}{(p_{j}^{-1}(U))}
(
j\in I
,
U\in{\mathcal{O}}{X_{j}}
)
of
X
by
\varsigma_{i}
is open in
X_{i}
. The elements in that
inverse image are the points
y\in X_{i}
such that

\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{k\in\upc i\cap\upc j}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{jk}}(\varsigma_{ik}(y))\subseteq U
. Since
X_{j}
is sober hence well-filtered, the latter
is equivalent to the existence of
k\in\upc i\cap\upc j
such
that
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{jk}}(\varsigma_{ik}(y))\subseteq U
. But

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{jk}}(\varsigma_{ik}(y))\subseteq U
is equivalent to

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{jk}}(\varsigma_{ik}(y))\in\Box_{0}U
, which is
equivalent to

y\in{({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{jk}}\circ\varsigma_{ik})}^{-1}(\Box_{0}U)
.
Therefore

\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}{(p_{j}^{-1}(U))})=\bigcup_{k\in\upc i\cap\upc j}{({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{jk}}\circ\varsigma_{ik})}^{-1}(\Box_{0}U)
, which is an open set.

Showing that
\varsigma_{i}
is quasi-adjoint to
p_{i}
is now a
formality.
For every
y\in X_{i}
,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(\varsigma_{i}(y))=\upc p_{i}[p_{i}^{-1}(\upc y)]\subseteq\upc y
, and for every
x\in X
,

\varsigma_{i}(p_{i}(x))=p_{i}^{-1}[\upc p_{i}(x)]
contains
x
. By
Lemma
12.4
, it follows that
p_{i}
is proper.

2. Since
\varsigma_{i}
is continuous,
\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}U)

is certainly open. For every
y\in\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}U)
,

\varsigma_{i}(y)=p_{i}^{-1}(\upc y)
is included in
U
. Since
open sets are upwards-closed,

\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}U)=\bigcup_{y\in\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}U)}\upc y
, so

p_{i}^{-1}(\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}U))=\bigcup_{y\in\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}U)}p_{i}^{-1}(\upc y)\subseteq U
.

Therefore
\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}U)\subseteq U_{i}
. In the
reverse direction, for every
y\in U_{i}
,

\varsigma_{i}(y)=p_{i}^{-1}(\upc y)\subseteq p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})\subseteq U
, so
\varsigma_{i}(y)\in\Box_{0}U
.

3. We have already defined a very similar function we called
h^{*}

in Lemma
12.1
, item
5
. But that
h^{*}
had

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
as domain, while the domain of
h^{\dagger}
is
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}h
. We
had shown that for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

{h^{*}}^{-1}(]t,\infty])=\Box{h^{-1}(]t,\infty])}
. It
immediately follows that

{h^{\dagger}}^{-1}(]t,\infty])=\Box_{0}{h^{-1}(]t,\infty])}
, a
basic open subset of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
. Hence
h^{\dagger}
is lower
semicontinuous, namely, in
\mathcal{L}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X}
.

Let us write
g
for
h^{\dagger}\circ\varsigma_{i}
, and
h_{i}
for
the largest function in
\mathcal{L}{X_{i}}
such that

h_{i}\circ p_{i}\leq h
, as described in Lemma
10.2
. We
have

g\circ p_{i}=h^{\dagger}\circ\varsigma_{i}\circ p_{i}\leq h^{\dagger}\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}
(by property (b) of quasi-adjoints and the fact
that continuous maps are monotonic), and

h^{\dagger}\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}=h
, since for every
x\in X
,

h^{\dagger}(\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}(x))=\min_{y\in\upc x}h(y)=h(x)
.
Since
h_{i}
is the largest element of
\mathcal{L}{X_{i}}
such that

h_{i}\circ p_{i}\leq h
,
g\leq h_{i}
. Conversely, the operation

\_^{\dagger}
is monotonic, so

g=h^{\dagger}\circ\varsigma_{i}\geq(h_{i}\circ p_{i})^{\dagger}\circ\varsigma_{i}
. For every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{0\mathsf{V}}X
, either
Q
is empty and

(h_{i}\circ p_{i})^{\dagger}(Q)=0=h_{i}^{\dagger}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(Q))
, or

Q
is non-empty and

(h_{i}\circ p_{i})^{\dagger}(Q)=\min_{x\in Q}h_{i}(p_{i}(x))=\min_{x\in Q,y\geq p_{i}(x)}h_{i}(y)=h_{i}^{\dagger}(\upc p_{i}[Q])=h_{i}^{\dagger}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}(Q))
. Therefore

(h_{i}\circ p_{i})^{\dagger}=h_{i}^{\dagger}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}
, and hence

g\geq h_{i}^{\dagger}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{p_{i}}\circ\varsigma_{i}\geq h_{i}^{\dagger}\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}
(by property (a) of quasi-adjoints)

=h_{i}
. Hence
g=h_{i}
. ∎

12.5
Projective limits of consonant and
\odot
-consonant
spaces

In a topological space
X
, for every compact saturated subset
Q
,
the collection
\blacksquare Q
of all open neighborhoods of
Q
is a
Scott-open subset of
{\mathcal{O}}X
. Any union of such sets

\blacksquare Q
is Scott-open, and
X
is called
consonant
if
and only if the converse holds, namely: for every Scott-open subset

\mathcal{U}
of
{\mathcal{O}}X
, for every
U\in\mathcal{U}
, there is a
compact saturated subset
Q
of
X
such that
Q\subseteq U
and

\blacksquare Q\subseteq\mathcal{U}
. As we said in
Section
4
, the notion arises from

[
8
]
; see also
[
18
, Exercise 5.4.12]
.

Proposition 12.13

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. If every
p_{ij}
is proper and if
every
X_{i}
is consonant and sober, then so is
X
.

Proof 46

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a Scott-open subset of
{\mathcal{O}}X
. For every

i\in I
, let
\mathcal{U}_{i}
be the collection of open subsets
U

of
X_{i}
such that
p_{i}^{-1}(U)\in\mathcal{U}
. Since
p_{i}^{-1}

commutes with unions,
\mathcal{U}_{i}
is Scott-open in
{\mathcal{O}}{X_{i}}
.
Now let
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. For each
i\in I
, let
U_{i}
be the
largest open subset of
X_{i}
such that
p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})\subseteq U
.
Then
{(p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i}))}_{i\in I,\sqsubseteq}
is a monotone net
of open subsets of
X
, whose union is
U
. Since
\mathcal{U}
is
Scott-open,
p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})\in\mathcal{U}
for some
i\in I
. In
other words,
U_{i}
is in
\mathcal{U}_{i}
.

We use the fact that
X_{i}
is consonant: there is a compact
saturated subset
Q_{i}
of
X_{i}
such that
Q_{i}\subseteq U_{i}
and
every open neighborhood of
Q_{i}
is in
\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Using
Proposition
12.12
, item 1,
Q\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}p_{i}^{-1}(Q_{i})
is
compact saturated in
X
.

We note that
Q\subseteq U
. Indeed, for every
x\in Q
,
p_{i}(x)\in Q_{i}\subseteq U_{i}
, so
x\in p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})\subseteq U
.

We claim that every open neighborhood
V
of
Q
in
X
lies in

\mathcal{U}
. Since
Q_{i}
is upwards-closed it is equal to the
union of the sets
\upc y
when
y
ranges over
Q_{i}
. Then

p_{i}^{-1}(\upc y)=\varsigma_{i}(y)
, where
\varsigma_{i}
is the
quasi-adjoint of
p_{i}
, so

Q=\bigcup_{y\in Q_{i}}\varsigma_{i}(y)
. The fact that

Q\subseteq V
then means that for every
y\in Q_{i}
,

\varsigma_{i}(y)\in\Box_{0}V
, hence that

Q_{i}\subseteq\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}V)
. By definition of

Q_{i}
,
\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}V)
is then in
\mathcal{U}_{i}
. By
Proposition
12.12
, item 2,
\varsigma_{i}^{-1}(\Box_{0}V)

is the largest open subset
V_{i}
of
X_{i}
such that

p_{i}^{-1}(V_{i})\subseteq V
. We have seen that

V_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
, so by definition of
\mathcal{U}_{i}
,

p_{i}^{-1}(V_{i})
is in
\mathcal{U}
. Since
\mathcal{U}
is
upwards-closed,
V
is in
\mathcal{U}
. ∎

For every topological space
X
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let the

copower

n\odot X
be the topological sum (categorical
coproduct) of
n
copies of
X
. In other words,
n\odot X
is the
collection of pairs
(k,x)
with
1\leq k\leq n
and
x\in X
,
with topology generated by the sets
\{k\}\times U
,
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
.
A space
X
is
\odot
-consonant
if and only if
n\odot X
is
consonant for every
n\in\mathbb{N}

[
6
, Definition 13.1]
. For example, every
LCS-complete space is
\odot
-consonant

[
6
, Lemma 13.2]
. There is an
n\odot\_

endofunctor on
\mathbf{Top}
: for every continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
,

n\odot f
maps every
(k,x)
to
(k,f(x))
.

A category
{\mathbf{I}}
is
connected
if and only if it has at least
one object, and every two objects are connected by a zig-zag of
morphisms. A connected diagram in a category
{\mathbf{C}}
is a functor
from a small connected category
{\mathbf{I}}
to
{\mathbf{C}}
. It is clear that
every projective system is a connected diagram. The following says
that the copower functor preserves connected limits in
\mathbf{Top}
.

Lemma 12.14

Let
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|}
be the canonical limit of a
connected diagram
F\colon{\mathbf{I}}\to\mathbf{Top}
. For every

n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\cdot X,{(n\odot p_{i})}_{i\in I}
is a projective
limit of
(n\odot\_)\circ F
.

Proof 47

Let
X^{\prime},{(q_{i})}_{i\in|{\mathbf{I}}|}
be the canonical projective limit
of
(n\odot\_)\circ F
. The elements of
X^{\prime}
are the tuples

{((k,x_{i}))}_{i\in I}
such that

(k,x_{i})=(n\odot p_{ij})(k,x_{j})
for all

i\sqsubseteq j\in I
. Note that the first component
k
must be
the same at all positions
i\in I
, because
{\mathbf{I}}
is connected.
A base of open subsets of
X^{\prime}
is given by the sets

p_{i}^{-1}(\{k\}\times U_{i})
, where
i\in I
,
1\leq k\leq n
, and

U_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}{X_{i}}
. The map

f\colon{((k,x_{i}))}_{i\in I}\mapsto(k,{(x_{i})}_{i\in I})
is
bijective. It is continuous and full because

f^{-1}(\{k\}\times p_{i}^{-1}(U_{i}))=p_{i}^{-1}(\{k\}\times U_{i})
. Hence
f
is a homeomorphism. Additionally, since
p_{i}
and

q_{i}
are both projections onto coordinate
i
,

(n\odot p_{i})\circ f=q_{i}
. ∎

Corollary 12.15

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. If every
p_{ij}
is proper and if
every
X_{i}
is
\odot
-consonant and sober, then so is
X
.

Proof 48

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\odot X,{(n\odot p_{i})}_{i\in I}
is a
projective limit of

(n\odot p_{ij}\colon n\odot X_{j}\to n\odot X_{i})_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
by Lemma
12.14
. By assumption each
space
n\odot X_{i}
is consonant. It is sober because any coproduct
of sober spaces taken in
\mathbf{Top}
is sober
[
18
, Lemma
8.4.2]
. By Proposition
12.13
,

n\odot X
must then be consonant. We finally recall that any
limit of sober spaces taken in
\mathbf{Top}
is sober. ∎

12.6
Projective limits of locally compact sober spaces are
consonant

An
\omega
-projective limit of locally compact sober spaces need not
be locally compact, even for compact, locally compact sober spaces

[
24
, Proposition 3.4]
. We will show that, while
local compactness is lost, the projective limit remains

\odot
-consonant.

Proposition 12.16

Let
{(p_{mn}\colon X_{n}\to X_{m})}_{m\leq n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. If every
X_{n}
is locally compact
and sober, then
X
is consonant.

Proof 49

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a Scott-open subset of
{\mathcal{O}}X
. For every

n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{U}_{n}
be the collection of open subsets

U
of
X_{n}
such that
p_{n}^{-1}(U)\in\mathcal{U}
. Since

p_{n}^{-1}
commutes with unions,
\mathcal{U}_{n}
is Scott-open in

{\mathcal{O}}{X_{n}}
. Now let
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

U_{n}
be the largest open subset of
X_{n}
such that

p_{n}^{-1}(U_{n})\subseteq U
. Then

{(p_{n}^{-1}(U_{n}))}_{n\in\mathbb{N},\leq}
is a monotone net of open
subsets of
X
, whose union is
U
. Since
\mathcal{U}
is
Scott-open,
p_{n}^{-1}(U_{n})\in\mathcal{U}
for
n
large enough.
In other words,
U_{n}
is in
\mathcal{U}_{n}
pour
n
large enough,
say
n\geq n_{0}
.

Since
X_{n_{0}}
is locally compact,
U_{n_{0}}
is a directed supremum
of sets
\text{int}({Q})
with
Q
compact saturated included in

U_{n_{0}}
. Since
\mathcal{U}_{n_{0}}
is Scott-open, one such set,
call it
\text{int}({Q_{n_{0}}})
, is in
\mathcal{U}_{n_{0}}
;
Q_{n_{0}}

is compact saturated and included in
U_{n_{0}}
. Then

p_{n_{0}(n_{0}+1)}^{-1}(\text{int}({Q_{n_{0}}}))
is in

\mathcal{U}_{n_{0}+1}
. Indeed, this means that

p_{n_{0}+1}^{-1}(p_{n_{0}(n_{0}+1)}^{-1}(\text{int}({Q_{n_{0}}})))\in\mathcal{U}
, equivalently, that

p_{n_{0}}^{-1}(\text{int}({Q_{n_{0}}}))\in\mathcal{U}
, namely that

\text{int}({Q_{n_{0}}})\in\mathcal{U}_{n_{0}}
. Since

\mathcal{U}_{n_{0}+1}
is Scott-open, there is a compact saturated
subset
Q_{n_{0}+1}
of
X_{n_{0}+1}
included in
\text{int}({Q_{n_{0}}})

whose interior is in
\mathcal{U}_{n_{0}+1}
. We proceed in the same
way for
n=n_{0}+2,n_{0}+3,\cdots
, and we obtain compact saturated
subsets
Q_{n}
of
X_{n}
for every
n\geq n_{0}
such that
\text{int}({Q_{n}})\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
and
Q_{n+1}\subseteq\text{int}({Q_{n}})
.
We complete this by letting
Q_{m}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc p_{mn_{0}}[Q_{n_{0}}]
for
every
m<n_{0}
.

We see each
Q_{n}
as a subspace of
X_{n}
. Since
X_{n}
is sober, by
Remark
5.2
,
Q_{n}
is sober. By construction,

{(p_{mn|Q_{n}}\colon Q_{n}\to Q_{m})}_{m\leq n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a
projective system, where
p_{mn|Q_{n}}
is the restriction of
p_{mn}

to
Q_{n}
, and it is a consequence of Steenrod’s theorem that its
canonical projective limit
Q
(or rather,

Q,{(p_{n|Q})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
) is compact saturated in
X
(and that
every compact saturated subset of
X
is obtained this way, see
Lemma 4.3 of
[
24
]
).

We claim that
Q\subseteq U
. For every

x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(x_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
Q
, we have
x_{n}\in Q_{n}
for
every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,

p_{n_{0}}(x)=x_{n_{0}}\in Q_{n_{0}}\subseteq U_{n_{0}}
, and since

p_{n_{0}}^{-1}(U_{n_{0}})\subseteq U
,
x\in U
.

We verify that every open neighborhood
V
of
Q
in
X
is in

\mathcal{U}
. Writing
V_{m}
for the largest open subset of
X
such
that
p_{m}^{-1}(V_{m})\subseteq V
, we have

V=\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}p_{m}^{-1}(V_{m})
. Since
Q
is compact,
Q

is included in
p_{m}^{-1}(V_{m})
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Equivalently,
p_{m}[Q]\subseteq V_{m}
, hence

\upc p_{m}[Q]\subseteq V_{m}
, since
V_{m}
is upwards-closed. By
Lemma
6.1
, there is an
n\geq m
such that

\upc p_{mn}[Q_{n}]\subseteq V_{m}
, so
p_{mn}[Q_{n}]\subseteq V_{m}
.
For every
n^{\prime}\geq n
, we have

p_{mn^{\prime}}[Q_{n^{\prime}}]=p_{mn}[p_{nn^{\prime}}[Q_{n^{\prime}}]]\subseteq p_{mn}[Q_{n}]\subseteq V_{m}
, so
p_{mn}[Q_{n}]\subseteq V_{m}
holds for
n
large
enough. We pick one such that
p_{mn}[Q_{n}]\subseteq V_{m}
, namely
such that
Q_{n}\subseteq p_{mn}^{-1}(V_{m})
, and
n\geq n_{0}
.
Since
n\geq n_{0}
, we know that
\text{int}({Q_{n}})\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
,
so
p_{mn}^{-1}(V_{m})\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
, since
\mathcal{U}_{n}
is
upwards-closed; therefore

p_{n}^{-1}(p_{mn}^{-1}(V_{m}))=p_{m}^{-1}(V_{m})
is in
\mathcal{U}
.
Since
p_{m}^{-1}(V_{m})\subseteq V
and
\mathcal{U}
is
upwards-closed,
V
is in
\mathcal{U}
.

Theorem 12.17

Let
{(p_{mn}\colon X_{n}\to X_{m})}_{m\leq n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. If every
X_{n}
is locally compact
and sober, then
X
is
\odot
-consonant and sober.

Proof 50

Any limit of sober spaces, taken in
\mathbf{Top}
, is sober

[
18
, Theorem 8.4.13]
. In order to see that
X
is

\odot
-consonant, we realize that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

k\odot X,{(k\odot p_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a projective limit of

{(k\odot p_{mn}\colon k\odot X_{n}\to k\odot X_{m})}_{m\leq n\in\mathbb{N}}
by Lemma
12.14
. Every finite copower of
locally compact sober spaces is locally compact sober. In fact, in

\mathbf{Top}
, every finite coproduct of locally compact spaces is
locally compact (an easy exercise), and every coproduct of sober
spaces is sober
[
18
, Lemma 8.4.2]
. We can now apply
Theorem
12.16
and we obtain that
k\odot X
is consonant.

13
Hoare powercones and sublinear previsions

We might think of proceeding in a similar way with the
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}

sublinear prevision functor as with the
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}
superlinear
prevision functor, but we will not. There is an analogue of the

(r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}},s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})
retraction, but it is only a natural
retraction on some subcategory
{\mathbf{K}}
of
\mathbf{Top}
consisting of

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly spaces. (We will define this notion below.)
Additionally, contrarily to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
, the
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
functor does not preserve
all projective limits of sober spaces.

Sublinear previsions form a model of mixed angelic non-deterministic
and probabilistic choice. Another, earlier model, due to

[
42
,
50
,
51
,
41
]
,
is the composition
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
, where

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)
is the subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)

consisting of convex non-empty closed sets. We start with the functor

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
. First, we verify that this is, indeed, a
functor.

We import the following from
[
34
]
. A
cone

is a set with a scalar multiplication operation, by scalars from

\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and with an addition operation, satisfying the expected laws.
A
semitopological cone
is a cone with a topology that makes
both scalar multiplication and addition separately continuous, where

\mathbb{R}_{+}
is given the Scott topology. For example,
\mathcal{L}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}X
,

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}X
,
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}X
are semitopological cones, and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
,
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
,
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
are
convex subspaces of the latter three. We
will need the following fact. In a semitopological cone, the closure of
a convex subsets is convex
[
34
, Lemma 4.10 (a)]
,
and we obtain the following as an easy consequence.

Fact 13.1

Given any convex subspace
Z
of a semitopological cone, the closure
of any convex subset of
Z
in
Z
is convex.

Lemma 13.2

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”. The

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
functor preserves convex combinations, namely: for
every continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
, for every
n\geq 1
, for
all non-negative real numbers
a_{1}
, …,
a_{n}
summing up to

1
, for all
\nu_{1},\cdots,\nu_{n}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\nu_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f(\nu_{i})
.

Proof 51

Both sides map every open subset
V
of
Y
to
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\nu_{i}(f^{-1}(V))
. ∎

Lemma 13.3

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”. The composition

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
is a functor from
\mathbf{Top}
to
\mathbf{Top}
,
whose action on morphisms is the restriction of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
.

Proof 52

Using Lemma
13.2
, for every

C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f[C]
is convex,
and therefore so is its closure
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f(C)
, by
Fact
13.1
. Hence
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f
maps elements

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
to elements of

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Y
, and we define
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f

as the corresponding restriction of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f
. This is a
continuous map, and the fact that
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
defines a
functor follows from the fact that
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
is a functor. ∎

Proposition 13.4

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. Let
\bullet
be nothing,
“
\leq 1
” or “
1
”.

Then

{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}

is a projective system of topological spaces, and

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X,{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is a projective limit of it provided that

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X,{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is the
projective limit of the projective system

{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
, up to
homeomorphism.

Proof 53

The fact that

{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}

is a projective system of topological spaces, and that

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X,{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is a cone on that system, follows from the fact that

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
is a functor
(Lemma
13.3
).

Let
Z,{(q_{i})}_{i\in I}
be the canonical limit of

{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
. We remember that

Z
is a space of
I
-indexed tuples, and that
q_{i}
is projection
onto coordinate
i
. The canonical limit of

{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}

is
Z^{\prime},{(q^{\prime}_{i})}_{i\in I}
where

Z^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{{(C_{i})}_{i\in I}\in Z\mid C_{i}\text{ is convex for
every }i\in I\}
, and
q^{\prime}_{i}
is the restriction of
q_{i}
to
Z^{\prime}
.
By assumption, there is homeomorphism

f\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\to Z
, defined by

f(C)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}p_{i}(C))}_{i\in I}
for every

C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
. By Lemma
13.3
, this
restricts to a continuous map

f^{\prime}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\to Z^{\prime}
. Since
f
is full, so
is
f^{\prime}
: every open subset of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
can be
written as
\mathcal{U}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
for some open
subset
\mathcal{U}
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
; since
f
is full,

\mathcal{U}=f^{-1}(\mathcal{V})
for some open subset
\mathcal{V}

of
Z
, and therefore

\mathcal{U}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X={f^{\prime}}^{-1}(\mathcal{V}\cap Z^{\prime})
. Since
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
is
T_{0}
(its
specialization preordering is inherited from its superspace

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, and is therefore the inclusion ordering),
f^{\prime}

is a topological embedding.

It remains to show that
f^{\prime}
is surjective. Let
{(C_{i})}_{i\in I}

be any element of
Z^{\prime}
; in particular, remember that
C_{i}
is closed
and convex. Since
f
is bijective, there is a unique non-empty
closed subset
C
of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that

C_{i}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}p_{i}(C)
for every
i\in I
. We claim that

C
is convex. In order to see this, we form the closure
C^{\prime}
of
the convex hull
\mathop{\text{conv}}C
of
C
; the
convex hull

\mathop{\text{conv}}C

is the smallest convex set containing
C
, and consists of the sums

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot x_{i}
where
n\geq 1
, the numbers
a_{i}
are
non-negative and sum up to
1
, and each
x_{i}
is in
C
.
C^{\prime}
is
closed and convex by Fact
13.1
. We will show that

C_{i}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}p_{i}(C^{\prime})
for every
i\in I
. Then, by
uniqueness of
C
, it will follows that
C=C^{\prime}
, so that
C
will
indeed be convex. Let us fix
i\in I
. Since
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
,

C_{i}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}p_{i}(C)\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}p_{i}(C^{\prime})
. In the reverse direction,

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}p_{i}(C^{\prime})=cl({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}p_{i}[cl(\mathop{\text{conv}}C)])\subseteq cl({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}p_{i}[\mathop{\text{conv}}C])
(since, for any
continuous map
f
, and for every set
A
,

f[cl(A)]\subseteq cl(f[A])
)

\subseteq cl(\mathop{\text{conv}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}p_{i}[C]))
(by our explicit
characterization of convex hulls and Lemma
13.2
)

\subseteq cl(\mathop{\text{conv}}C_{i})=C_{i}
, where the last equality is because

C_{i}
is closed and convex.

Now
f^{\prime}
is a surjective topological embedding, hence a
homeomorphism. Additionally,

q^{\prime}_{i}\circ f^{\prime}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{i}}
for every
i\in I
,
since
q_{i}\circ f={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{i}}
. ∎

Theorem 13.5

Let
{(p_{ij}\colon X_{j}\to X_{i})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
be a
projective system of topological spaces, with canonical projective
limit
X,{(p_{i})}_{i\in I}
. Let
\bullet
be nothing,
“
\leq 1
” or “
1
”.
If:

1.

the projective system is an ep-system,

2.

or
I
has a countable cofinal subset and each
X_{i}
is
locally compact sober (and compact if
\bullet
is “
1
”),

3.

or every
X_{i}
is consonant sober and every
p_{ij}
is a
proper map,

then

{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}

is a projective system of topological spaces, and

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X,{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is its projective limit, up to homeomorphism.

Case 3 in particular applies when every
X_{i}
is LCS-complete;
LCS-complete spaces are even
\odot
-consonant

[
6
, Lemma 13.2]
, and they are sober

[
6
, Proposition 7.1]
.

Proof 54

In all cases,

{({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
is a projective system of
topological spaces, and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X,{({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is its projective
limit, up to homeomorphism, by Theorem
4.3
.

We claim that

{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
is a projective
system of topological spaces, and

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X,{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{i}})}_{i\in I}
is its
projective limit, up to homeomorphism. This will allow us to
conclude by Proposition
13.4
. In order to show
the claim, we rely on Theorem
7.5
; let us
check its assumptions.

In case 1,

{({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{ij}}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{j}}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}})}_{i\sqsubseteq j\in I}
is an ep-system. Indeed, the
image of an ep-system by any monotonic functor is an ep-system.
Therefore case 1 of Theorem
7.5
applies. In case 2,
every space
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}}
is locally compact and sober by
Theorem
12.2
(this is why we require
X_{i}
to be
compact when
\bullet
is “
1
”), so case 3 of
Theorem
7.5
applies. In case 3, every space

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{X_{i}}
is sober (see Remark
5.2
), and
every map
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{p_{ij}}
is proper, by
Theorem
12.11
, so case 2 of Theorem
7.5

applies. ∎

In a semitopological cone, scalar multiplication is always jointly
continuous, but addition may fail to be. Let us introduce more
material from
[
34
]
. A
topological cone
is
one where addition is jointly continuous. A semitopological cone
C

is
locally convex
if and only if for every
x\in C
, every
open neighborhood of
x
contains a convex open neighborhood of
x
.
It is
locally convex-compact
if and only if for every

x\in C
, every open neighborhood of
x
contains a convex compact
saturated neighborhood of
x
.

A space
X
is
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly

[
23
, Definition 1]

if and only if:

—

\bullet
is nothing or “
\leq 1
”, and
\mathcal{L}X
is locally
convex;

—

or
\bullet
is “
1
”, and either:

1.

\mathcal{L}X
is locally convex and
X
is compact;

2.

or
\mathcal{L}X
is a locally convex, locally convex-compact,
sober topological cone;

3.

or
X
is LCS-complete.

We recall that
\mathcal{L}X
is equipped with its Scott topology.

Every core-compact space is
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly, for any value of

\bullet

[
23
, Remark 2]
.
Hence, in particular, every locally compact space is

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly. Every LCS-complete space is

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly for any value of
\bullet
, and

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{1}
-friendliness implies
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
-friendliness

[
23
, Remark 3]
.
Also, every LCS-complete space is

\odot
-consonant
[
6
, Lemma 13.2]
, and

\mathcal{L}X
is locally convex for every
\odot
-consonant space
X
.
We summarize all this as follows.

Fact 13.6

Every core-compact space and in particular every locally compact
space, every LCS-complete space and in particular every

\odot
-consonant space is
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
-friendly (and

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\leq 1}
-friendly). Every core-compact space, every locally
compact space, every LCS-complete space, every compact

\odot
-consonant space is
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{1}
-friendly.

We turn to sublinear previsions. From

[
19
, Proposition 3.11]
(and its errata

[
23
]
), there is a map

r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X)\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
and a map
s_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}
in the other direction, defined by

r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}(C)(h)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sup_{G\in C}G(h)
for every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
and

s_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{G\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X\mid G\leq F\}
, and they form a retraction under the assumption that
X
is

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly.

For any
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly space
X
,
r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}
restricts to a
homeomorphism, with inverse
s_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}
, between the subspace

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X)\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
of
non-empty closed convex subsets of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
and

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
, see Theorem 4.11 of
[
19
]
and its
errata
[
23
]
.

We write
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
for the transformation consisting of all the maps

r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}
, when
X
varies, and similarly with
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
.

Lemma 13.7

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or “
1
”. The
transformations
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
restrict to natural
transformations between
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}

(resp., natural isomorphisms between
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
and

\mathbb{P}^{\bullet}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
) on the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Top}
consisting
of
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly spaces.

Proof 55

We will need to use the following observation:
(*)
for any lower
semicontinuous map
\psi\colon Z\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, where
Z
is any
topological space, for every
A\subseteq Z
,

\sup_{z\in A}\psi(z)=\sup_{z\in cl(A)}\psi(z)
. Indeed,
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
t<\sup_{z\in A}\psi(z)
if and only
if
\psi^{-1}(]t,\infty])
intersects
A
,

t<\sup_{z\in A}\psi(z)
if and only if

\psi^{-1}(]t,\infty])
intersects
cl(A)
, and those are
equivalent conditions since
\psi^{-1}(]t,\infty])
is open.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be any continuous map, where both
\mathcal{L}X

and
\mathcal{L}Y
are locally convex. Let us start with
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
. We
need to show that for every
C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X)
,
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}Y
,

r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f)(C))(h)=\mathbb{P}f(r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}(C))(h)
.
The left-hand side is equal to

\sup_{G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f)(Q)}G^{\prime}(h)=\sup_{G^{\prime}\in cl(\{\mathbb{P}f(G)\mid G\in C\})}G^{\prime}(h)=\sup_{G^{\prime}\in\{\mathbb{P}f(G)\mid G\in C\}}G^{\prime}(h)
(by
(*)
, since
G^{\prime}\mapsto G^{\prime}(h)
is lower
semicontinuous, by definition of the weak topology)

=\sup_{G\in C}\mathbb{P}f(G)(h)=\sup_{G\in C}G(h\circ f)=r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}(C)(h\circ f)=\mathbb{P}f(r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}(C))(h)
.

As far as
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is concerned, we must show that for every

F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
,

s_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{P}f(F))={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f)\allowbreak(s_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F))
. The left-hand side is convex, and we
claim that the right-hand side is, too. Knowing this, we will be
able to conclude: since
r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}
restricted to

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X)
is a homeomorphism, it is
enough to show that

r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}(s_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{P}f(F)))=r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,Y}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f)\allowbreak(s_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F)))
, and this will
follow from the naturality of
r_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}
.

Hence it remains to show that

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}(\mathbb{P}f)\allowbreak(s_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F))
is convex.
This is equal to
cl(A)
, where

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\mathbb{P}f(G)\mid G\in s_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\,X}^{\bullet}(F)\}
